Existence, uniqueness and ergodicity for the centered Fleming-Viot process
Nicolas Champagnat

Abstract




Abstract.
Motivated by questions of ergodicity for shift invariant
Fleming-Viot
process, we consider the centered
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined by
Z_{t}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}
, where
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is the original
Fleming-Viot
process. Our goal is to characterise the centered
Fleming-Viot
process with a martingale problem.
To establish the existence of a solution to this martingale problem, we exploit the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem and asymptotic expansions. The proof of uniqueness is based on a weakened version of the duality method, allowing us to prove uniqueness for initial conditions admitting finite moments. We also provide counter examples showing that our approach based on the duality method cannot be expected to give uniqueness for more general initial conditions. Finally, we establish ergodicity properties with exponential convergence in total variation for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process and characterise the invariant measure.

Keywords.
Measure-valued diffusion processes,
Fleming-Viot
processes, Martingale problems,
Duality method, Exponential ergodicity in total variation,
Donnelly-Kurtz
’s modified look-down.

MSC subject classification.
Primary 37A25, 37A30, 60G44, 60J60, 60J68; Secondary 60B10, 60G09, 60J76, 60J90, 92D10.

1
Introduction

Fleming
and
Viot
have introduced in

[
27
]
a probability-measure-valued stochastic process modeling the dynamics of the distribution of allelic frequencies in a
selectively neutral
genetic population as influenced by mutation and random genetic drift: the original
Fleming-Viot
process. The initial model of

[
27
]
was progressively enriched with further mechanisms of Darwinian evolution: selection

[
27
,
21
,
23
,
9
,
18
]
, recombination

[
25
,
23
]
or the effect of an environment

[
28
]
.
Fleming
and
Viot
characterise in

[
27
]
the law of their process as a solution of a
Stroock-Varadhan
measure-valued martingale problem

[
51
]
both in the selective neutral case and the case with selection. To obtain the existence of such solution on a compact metric space, their method is based on discretisation of the mutation operator and tightness arguments.
An alternative approach is used, in the studies

[
40
,
41
,
32
,
31
,
50
]
based on the
Otha-Kimura
model

[
42
,
43
]
and in the references

[
27
,
10
,
23
,
17
]
based on its continuous-time version: the
Moran
model (also called
continuous-state stepwise mutation model
). If we denote by
N
the population size, these authors construct a particle process whose limiting behavior is analysed under the assumptions that the mutation step is proportional to
1/\sqrt{N}
and on the time scale
(Nt)_{t\geqslant 0}
. In

[
28
]
, another particle process, based on the lookdown construction

[
14
]
is used to show the existence of the
Fleming-Viot
process in a random environment. This lookdown construction also allows to analyse sample path properties of the process and has been used in numerous references since then, such as

[
15
]

[
17
,
Chapter 5]
.

In

[
27
]
, uniqueness of the solution to the
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem in the selectively neutral case, is proved using uniqueness of moments of certain finite-dimensional distributions and arguments on semigroup. However, in the case where natural selection acts, the previous method fails, but the result can be obtained from a version of the
Cameron-Martin-Girsanov
formula

[
9
,
Chapter 10]

[
8
,
Theorem 5.1]
. See also
[
24
]
for an application of this method in the case of unbounded selection function. In most references such as

[
17
,
10
,
20
,
21
,
23
,
24
]
, under a variety of assumptions, the
duality method

[
26
,
Proposition 4.4.7]
is used to prove the uniqueness of the
Fleming-Viot
process. The idea is to relate the distribution of the original process with that of a simple process, called
dual process
. This leads to a duality relation which ensures that two solutions to the martingale problem have the same 1-dimensional marginal laws. Uniqueness of the solution to the martingale problem then follows from
Markov
’s property

[
26
,
Theorem 4.4.2]
. Other methods are used in some references:

[
11
]
makes use of resolvent estimates;

[
45
,
44
]
prove existence and uniqueness of
Fleming-Viot
processes with
unbounded selection
intensity functions by using
Dirichlet
’s forms.

Questions of ergodicity of the
Fleming-Viot
process were also the subject of many works. Let
E
be a Polish space and
{\mathcal{B}}(E)
the
Borel

\sigma-
field on
E
. Let us recall from

[
23
,
Section 5]
that an
E-
valued
Markov
process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
with a unique stationary distribution
\pi
is
weakly ergodic
if for all bounded continuous functions
f
on
E
, for all initial condition
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{E}}
,

\lim_{t\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{x_{0}}}\left(f\left(Z_{t}\right)\right)=\int_{E}{f(x){\pi}(\mathrm{d}x)}}

(1)

and
strongly ergodic
if

\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\sup_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}(E)}{\left|{\mathbb{P}}_{{x_{0}}}\left(Z_{t}\in B\right)-{\pi}(B)\right|}}=0,\quad{x_{0}}\in E.

(2)

If
E
is compact, for mutation operators
A
whose closure generates a
Feller
semigroup on the space of continuous functions and such that there is a unique probability measure
\nu_{0}
on
E
satisfying
\int_{E}{Af(x)\nu_{0}(\mathrm{d}x)}=0
, some ergodicity results for the
Fleming-Viot
process are obtained in

[
23
]
. More precisely, in the selectively neutral case and without recombination, a simple proof of weak ergodicity of the
Fleming-Viot
process is given using duality arguments whereas coupling arguments provide an approach to strong ergodicity. These results were extended in

[
22
]
to models with recombination and in

[
24
]
to models with unbounded selection, with the additional tool of
Dawson
’s
Girsanov
-type formula for strong ergodicity. In the special case where the mutation operator of the
Fleming-Viot
process has the form

Af(x)=\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{E}{\left(f(y)-f(x)\right)P(x,\mathrm{d}y)},\quad\theta\in(0,+\infty),\ f\in{\mathcal{D}}(A),

(3)

it is proved in

[
23
]
that the
Fleming-Viot
process has a reversible stationary distribution if
P(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\nu(\mathrm{d}y)
for some probability measure
\nu
on
E
(see

[
35
]
for a converse result). For the mutation operator (
3
), it is proved in

[
20
,
21
]
and
[
9
,
Theorem 8.2.1]
that the
Fleming-Viot
process is purely atomic for every time, in other words the solutions of the martingale problem take values in the set of purely atomic probability measures. In

[
25
]
, the ergodicity result of

[
22
]
was extended to the weak atomic topology.

However, if we consider the case where the mutation operator is the Laplacian on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there exists no stationary distribution

[
23
,
35
]
,

[
26
,
Problem 11 p.450]
. Instead the process exhibits a wandering phenomenon

[
10
,
3
]
. Nevertheless,

[
50
,
23
]
considered the
Fleming-Viot
process shifted by minus its empirical mean and established existence of a unique invariant measure and weak ergodicity for this process using moment and duality arguments. More precisely in

[
23
]
, thanks to some estimates of the original
Fleming-Viot
dual process and the finiteness of all moments of the
Fleming-Viot
process shifted by minus its empirical mean for any time
t
, the authors obtain an expression for these in the asymptotic
t\to+\infty
. Then, by tightness arguments and characterisation of the limit, the result follows. In

[
50
]
, an analoguous approach is used for the continuous-state stepwise mutation model.

In this paper we are interested in the
Fleming-Viot
process shifted by minus its empirical mean, which we call
centered
Fleming-Viot
process
. As in previous works it is natural to ask questions of existence, uniqueness and ergodicity when the mutation operator is the Laplacian on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Moreover, the study of this process was motivated by biological questions in adaptive dynamics. The theory of adaptative dynamics

[
39
]
is based on biological assumptions of
rare
and
small mutations
and of
large population
under which an ODE approximating the population evolutionary dynamics, the
Canonical Equation of Adaptive Dynamics
(CEAD) was proposed
[
12
]
. Two mathematical approaches were developed to give a proper mathematical justification of this theory: a
deterministic
one

[
13
,
47
,
38
]
, and a
stochastic
one

[
4
,
7
,
6
]
. Despite their success, the proposed approaches are criticised by biologists
[
52
,
48
]
. Among the biological assumptions of adaptive dynamics, the assumption of rare mutations is the most critised as unrealistic. In order to solve this problem, we propose to apply an asymptotic of small mutations and large population, but
frequent mutations
. After conveniently scaling the population state, this leads to a
slow-fast dynamics

[
46
,
34
]
, where the fast dynamics appears to be given by a discrete version of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process

[
5
]
. This explains why we are interested in ergodicity properties of such processes.

To establish the existence of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process, we characterise it as a solution of a measure-valued martingale problem that we called the
centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem
. Our method is to exploit the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem and asymptotic expansions. An additional difficulty occurs in our case since we need to apply the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem to
predictable test functions
. This requires to extend the martingale problem to such test functions using
regular conditional probabilities
. The proof of uniqueness of the solution of the centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem is based on duality methods as in the previous works. However, additional difficulties occur in our case since bounds on the dual process are much harder to obtain and the duality identity can only be proved in a weakened version. In particular, our uniqueness result only holds for initial conditions admitting finite moments. We also provide a counter example showing that our uniqueness result is optimal in the sense that we cannot expect to obtain uniqueness for more general initial conditions using the duality approach. Finally, we obtain strong ergodicity properties of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process that extend the weak ergodicity results obtained in

[
50
,
23
]
. To this aim, we construct the centered version of the
Moran
process and we prove that it converges in law to the centered
Fleming-Viot
process. Exploiting the relationship between the
Moran
model and the
Kingman
coalescent, we obtain a result of exponential ergodicity in total variation for the centered
Moran
model uniformly in the number of particles. This result is propagated to the centered
Fleming-Viot
process by coupling arguments. Using another strategy proposed by

[
50
,
23
]
, based on the
Donnelly-Kurtz
modified look-down

[
15
]
we give a characterisation of the unique invariant measure of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process.

This paper is organised as follows. In Section
2
we define the martingale problem for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process and establish an existence result. We give also some equivalent extensions to the centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem and some properties of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process. In Section
3
we prove uniqueness to the centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem for initial conditions admitting finite moments and we discuss this assumption. In Section
4
, we establish exponential convergence in total variation for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process to its unique invariant measure and provide a characterisation of this measure based on the
Donnelly-Kurtz
modified look-down. Finally in Sections
5
and
6
, we prove respectively the main results of existence and uniqueness of the solution of the centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem. The paper ends with appendices gathering technical lemmas for the existence proof and extensions to the centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem.

2
Existence for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process

In this section, our aim is to define the martingale problem for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process and to establish an existence result. This result is stated in Section
2.1
. Then, we give in Section
2.2
, the framework and ideas of the proof. We end this section by giving some interesting results about the centered
Fleming-Viot
process: it satisfies the
Markov
property (Section
2.3.1
), admits finite moments (Section
2.3.2
) and has compact support (Section
2.3.3
).

2.1
Centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem and main result

The centered
Fleming-Viot
process is a measure-valued diffusion in

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right):=\left\{\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right.\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)}<\infty,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{x\mu(\mathrm{d}x)}=0\right\}

which is endowed with the trace of the topology of weak convergence on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
, the set of probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
endowed with the topology of weak convergence making it a Polish space

[
2
]
. We consider the filtered probability space
\left({\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}},\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}},\left(\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\right)
where

\forall d\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star},\quad\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}:=\left\{X\in{\mathscr{C}}\left(\left[0,+\infty\right),{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\right)\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right.\forall T>0,\ \sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\left\|x\right\|^{2}X_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)}<\infty}\right\}

is endowed with the trace of the
Skorohod
topology on
\Omega_{d}:={\mathscr{C}}([0,+\infty),{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
, the set of continuous functions from
[0,+\infty)
to
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}
is the trace of the
Borel

\sigma-
field
{\mathcal{F}}
on
\Omega_{d}
and
(\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is the trace of the
Borel
filtration
({\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\Omega_{d}
. Let us denote
\widetilde{\Omega}:=\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}
.

We introduce several notations that we use repeatedly in the sequel. For a measurable real bounded function
f
and a measure
\nu\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)}
, we denote
\left\langle f,\nu\right\rangle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{f(x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)}.
We denote by
{\rm{id}}=({\rm{id}}_{1},\cdots,{\rm{id}}_{d})
the identity function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
where for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
,
{\rm{id}}_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}},x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})\mapsto x_{k}
. We denote
{\mathbb{N}}:=\left\{0,1,2,\cdots\right\}
and
{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}:={\mathbb{N}}\setminus{\{0\}}
. If
{\mathcal{D}}
is a domain of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, then for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{\ell}({\mathcal{D}},{\mathbb{R}})
the space of functions of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{\ell}
from
{\mathcal{D}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
. For
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{\ell}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
the space of real bounded functions of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{\ell}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
with bounded derivatives. For all
F\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
we denote for all
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
,
F_{g}(\nu):=F\left(\left\langle g,\nu\right\rangle\right)
.
We denote by
\Delta
the Laplacian operator on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and by
x^{t}
the transpose vector of
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. For any function
f
whose second partial derivatives exist, we denote by
{\rm{Hess}}(f):=\left(\partial_{ij}^{2}f\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}
the Hessian matrix of
f
.

Definition 2.1
.

A probability measure on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{d})
is said to solve the
centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem
with resampling rate
\gamma\in(0,+\infty)
and with initial condition
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
if the canonical process
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}(X_{0}=\mu)=1
and for each
F\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
,

\widehat{M}_{t}^{F,d}(g):=F_{g}\left(X_{t}\right)-F_{g}\left(X_{0}\right)-\int_{0}^{t}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{d}F_{g}\left(X_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s}

(4)

with for all
\varpi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{d}F_{g}(\varpi)

\displaystyle:=F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle\frac{\Delta g}{2},\varpi\right\rangle+\gamma\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{t}\left\langle{\rm{Hess}}\left(g\right),\varpi\right\rangle{\rm{id}},\varpi\right\rangle-2\left\langle\nabla g^{t}{\rm{id}},\varpi\right\rangle\right]\right)

(5)

\displaystyle+\gamma F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle g^{2},\varpi\right\rangle-\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle\nabla g,\varpi\right\rangle^{t}\left\langle{\rm{id}}\,{\rm{id}}^{t},\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla g,\varpi\right\rangle-2\left\langle\nabla g,\varpi\right\rangle^{t}\left\langle g\times{\rm{id}},\varpi\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\frac{\partial_{ii}^{2}g}{2},\varpi\right\rangle}+\gamma\left[\sum_{i,j\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{ij}^{2}g,\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}_{i}\times{\rm{id}}_{j},\varpi\right\rangle}-2\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{i}g\times{\rm{id}}_{i},\varpi\right\rangle}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\gamma F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle g^{2},\varpi\right\rangle-\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i,j\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{i}g,\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle\partial_{j}g,\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}_{i}\times{\rm{id}}_{j},\varpi\right\rangle}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.2\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{i}g,\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle g\times{\rm{id}}_{i},\varpi\right\rangle}\right)

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale in
L^{2}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}\right)
with quadratic variation process

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{M}^{F,d}(g)\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{\left[F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,X_{s}\right\rangle\right)\right]^{2}\left[\left\langle g^{2},X_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle g,X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right.}

(6)

\displaystyle+\left.\left\langle\nabla g,X_{s}\right\rangle^{t}\left\langle{\rm{id}}\,{\rm{id}}^{t},X_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla g,X_{s}\right\rangle-2\left\langle\nabla g,X_{s}\right\rangle^{t}\left\langle g\times{\rm{id}},X_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s.

Remark 2.2
.

Defining

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}):=\Big\{\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\,\Big|\,\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2},\nu\right\rangle<\infty\Big\}

the above definition takes a simpler form in the case
d=1
. Indeed, defining
for all
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})

M_{2}\left(\nu\right):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\left|x-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle\right|^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}x)},

we notice that
M_{2}(\nu)=\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},\nu\rangle
if
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then, with the notations of
Definition
6
, the generator of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}F_{g}(\varpi)

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\rm FVc}F_{g}(\varpi)=F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{2},\varpi\right\rangle+\gamma\left[\left\langle g^{\prime\prime},\varpi\right\rangle M_{2}(\varpi)-2\left\langle g^{\prime}\times{\rm{id}},\varpi\right\rangle\right]\right)

(7)

\displaystyle+\gamma F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle g^{2},\varpi\right\rangle-\left\langle g,\varpi\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle g^{\prime},\varpi\right\rangle^{2}M_{2}\left(\varpi\right)-2\left\langle g^{\prime},\varpi\right\rangle\left\langle g\times{\rm{id}},\varpi\right\rangle\right).

The original
Fleming-Viot
process is a measure-valued diffusion in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
. We consider the filtered probability space
\left({\Omega_{d}},{\mathcal{F}},\left({\mathcal{F}}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\right)
as defined above.
We recall that a probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left(\Omega_{d}\right)}
is said to solve the
original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem
with resampling rate
\gamma
and initial condition
\nu\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
if the canonical process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\Omega_{d}
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}(Y_{0}=\nu)=1
and for each
F\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}},{\mathbb{R}})
,

\displaystyle M_{t}^{F}(g)

\displaystyle:=F\left(\left\langle g,Y_{t}\right\rangle\right)-F\left(\left\langle g,Y_{0}\right\rangle\right)-\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Y_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle\frac{\Delta g}{2},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}

(8)

\displaystyle-\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Y_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left[\left\langle g^{2},Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle g,Y_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}s}

is a continuous square integrable
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale whose bracket satisfies for all
G,H\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and for all
g,h\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
,

\left\langle M^{G}(g),M^{H}(h)\right\rangle_{t}=2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{G^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Y_{s}\right\rangle\right)H^{\prime}\left(\left\langle h,Y_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left[\left\langle gh,Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle g,Y_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle h,Y_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s}.

(9)

In the case
d=1
, we will denote
\Omega:=\Omega_{1}
.

In the population genetics literature, the terms involving the first order derivative
F^{\prime}
in (
8
) describe the effect of the mutation whereas the ones involving the second order derivative
F^{\prime\prime}
describe the effect of the random genetic drift. It is well-known that, for all
\nu\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
, there exists a unique probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left(\Omega_{d}\right)}
satisfying the previous martingale problem (
8
)
[
27
,
Theorem 3]
.

Remark 2.3
.

(1)

The additional terms in the martingale problem
(
4
)
with respect to the martingale problem
(
8
)
describe the impact of centering and ensure that at all times the centered
Fleming-Viot
process remains
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})-
valued.

(2)

Note that a major difficulty comes from the presence of terms in
\langle{\rm{id}}_{i}\times{\rm{id}}_{j},\mu\rangle
,
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
in
(
5
)
or
M_{2}(\mu)
in
(
7
)
. We will see in particular that this leads to the creation of particles in the dual process.

Let us define by
\tau_{\alpha}
the translation operator of vector
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. For all
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
, for all
A\in{\mathcal{B}}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
,

\tau_{\alpha}\sharp\,\mu(A):=\mu\left(\tau_{\alpha}^{-1}(A)\right)=\mu\left(\left\{x-\alpha\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right.x\in A\right\}\right),

where
\sharp
is the
pushforward operator
.

The main result of this section is the following:

Theorem 2.4
.

For all
\mu\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)}
, there exists a probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\in{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}\right)}
satisfying the martingale problem of
Definition
6
, given by the law of the process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined by

Z_{t}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}:=Y_{t}\left(\cdot+\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle\right),\qquad t\geqslant 0

(10)

where
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is the original
Fleming-Viot
process.

Note that, for all probability measure with finite first-order moment,
\langle{\rm{id}},\mu\rangle
is the mean value (or barycenter) of
\mu
. In addition,
\left\langle{\rm{id}},Z_{t}\right\rangle=0
for all
t\geqslant 0
. As a result, the process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
corresponds to the original
Fleming-Viot
process centered by its mean value, hence its name of
centered
Fleming-Viot
process
.

2.2
Sketch of proof of Theorem
2.4

The proof of Theorem
2.4
, for any dimension
d\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
is much more complicated, but it is treated in the same way as for dimension
1
. In the following, only the case where
d=1
is detailed for greater clarity. The proof is based on the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem (
8
). Below, we give the main ideas of the proof. Full details are provided in Section
5
.

2.2.1
Framework and objective of the proof

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}
the unique solution to the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem (
8
). As the support of the original
Fleming-Viot
process is compact at all positive times
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
a.s.

[
37
]
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}\left({\mathscr{C}}^{0}\left(\left[0,+\infty\right),{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\right)\right)=1
.
Moreover, as
t\mapsto\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle
is continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
a.s. (Lemma
5.1

(2)
), we deduce that, for all
t\geqslant 0
,
Z_{t}
given by (
10
), is well defined and is a random variable on
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

When the dependency of
Y
and
Z
on the initial condition
\nu
of
Y
is important, we shall use the notation
\left(Y_{t}^{\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
\left(Z_{t}^{\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
instead of
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. Our goal is to prove that the law of the process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
denoted by
{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu}^{\rm FVc}
solves the martingale problem of Definition
6
with initial condition
\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu
. Note that the notation
{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu}^{\rm FVc}
is justified
because the original
Fleming-Viot
process is invariant by translation:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
and
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, the law of
Z_{t}^{\nu}
defined by
(
10
)
is the same as the law of
Z_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}
.

Proof.

By translation invariance of the original
Fleming-Viot
process, the process
\left(\tau_{-a}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
has the same law as the process
\left(Y_{t}^{\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. Now,

\displaystyle Z_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\tau_{-a}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}\right\rangle}\sharp\,\left(\tau_{-a}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\,\nu}\right).

Thus,
\left(Z_{t}^{\tau_{a}\sharp\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
has the same law as
\left(\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}^{\nu}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}=\left(Z_{t}^{\nu}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
.
∎

2.2.2
Outline of the proof

We restrict to the time interval
[0,T]
for
T>0
arbitrary. By standard arguments, it is sufficient to prove that, for all
F\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
,

F_{g}\left(Z_{t}\right)-F_{g}\left(Z_{0}\right)-\int_{0}^{t}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}F_{g}\left(Z_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s}

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale,
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
where
{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}
is given by (
7
). We start by assuming
F,g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{4}_{b}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and we seek for the
Doob
’s semi-martingale decomposition of

\displaystyle F_{g}(Z_{t}):=F\left(\left\langle g,Z_{t}\right\rangle\right)=F\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle},Y_{t}\right\rangle\right),

using the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem (
8
). However,
F\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle},Y_{t}\right\rangle\right)

does not take the form
H\left(\left\langle h,Y_{t}\right\rangle\right)
with
deterministic

h
. Therefore, we cannot apply (
8
) directly. To get over this difficulty, we consider for
t\in[0,T]
,
an increasing sequence
0=t_{0}^{n}<t_{1}^{n}<\dots<t_{p_{n}}^{n}=T
of subdivisions of
[0,T]
whose mesh tends to 0. We can observe that

\displaystyle F_{g}(Z_{t})-F_{g}(Z_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{\left\{F_{g}(Z_{t^{n}_{i+1}\wedge t})-F_{g}(Z_{t^{n}_{i}\wedge t})\right\}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{p_{n}-1}{\left\{F\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)-F\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i\phantom{+1}\hskip-7.11317pt}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)\right\}.}

Using asymptotic expansions (see Lemma
A.1
with
p=d=1
) of the terms in the previous sum, we prove that

\displaystyle F_{g}(Z_{t})-F_{g}(Z_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{}\left\{{\rm{\textbf{(A)}}}_{i}+{\rm{\textbf{(B)}}}_{i}+O\left(\left|\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right|^{3}\right)\phantom{\sum_{k}^{2}{}}\right.

(11)

\displaystyle+\left.O\left(\sum\limits_{k\,=\,0}^{2}{\left|\left\langle g^{(k)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right|^{3}}\right)\right\},

where

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{(A)}}}_{i}=\

\displaystyle F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)\left\{\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-38.41139pt-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\left[\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle+\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right]

(12)

\displaystyle\hskip-38.41139pt+\left.\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle^{2}\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right\},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{(B)}}}_{i}=\

\displaystyle\frac{F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)}{2}\left\{\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-38.41139pt+\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle^{2}\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle^{2}

(13)

\displaystyle\hskip-38.41139pt-\left.2\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle^{\phantom{2}}\hskip-5.69046pt\right\},

and where
g^{(j)}
,
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, denotes the
j^{\rm{th}}
derivative of
g
. Note that the proposed decomposition in (
11
) is intended such that each of the terms in (
12
) and (
13
) is either
{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}-
adapted or, exhibit increments of
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
between
t_{i}^{n}\wedge t
and
t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t
. Several steps are described in Section
5
to obtain the semi-martingale decomposition of each term of the previous sum. Note that the last terms of
{\rm{\textbf{(A)}}}_{i}
and
{\rm{\textbf{(B)}}}_{i}
bring out the terms in
M_{2}(\mu)
. By making the step of the subdivision tend towards 0, we obtain the expected result.

2.3
Some properties of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process in dimension
1

2.3.1
Markov
’s property

Due to the translation invariance property of the original
Fleming-Viot
process, we can prove that the centered
Fleming-Viot
process is homogeneous
Markov
.

Proposition 2.6
.

The centered
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined by
(
10
)
satisfies the homogeneous
Markov
property: for all measurable bounded function
f
,

\forall\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}}),\quad\forall t,s>0,\quad\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f(Z_{t+s})\left|\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-5.69046pt{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\right.\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{t}}\left(f(Z_{s})\right)\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}{\rm{-a.s}}.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})
and
f
a measurable bounded function. Let
t,s>0
. Using the
Markov
property of the original
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
we obtain
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
a.s.,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f(Z_{t+s})\left|\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-5.69046pt{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\right.\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f(\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t+s}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t+s})\left|\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-5.69046pt{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\right.\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(g(Y_{t+s})\left|\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-5.69046pt{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\right.\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{Y_{t}}\left(g(Y_{s})\right)

where the bounded measurable map
g
is defined on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
by
g(\nu):=f\left(\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu\right)
. By invariance by translation of the original
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
we obtain under the distribution
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{Y_{t}}\left(g\left(Y_{s}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}}\left(g\left(\tau_{\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{s}\right)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}}\left(g\left(Y_{s}\right)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{t}}\left(g\left(Y_{s}\right)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{Z_{t}}\left(f\left(Z_{s}\right)\right).\qed

2.3.2
Moments and some martingales

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})
and let
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}
be a distribution on
\widetilde{\Omega}
satisfying
(
4
)
and such that
X_{0}
is equal in law to
\mu
. Let
T>0
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\backslash\,\{0,1\}
be fixed.

(1)

If
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
, there exist two constants
C_{k,T},\widetilde{C}_{k,T}>0
, such that any stochastic process
\left(X_{t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
whose law is
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}
satisfies

(a)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle\right)}\leqslant C_{k,T}\left(1+\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\mu\right\rangle\right)

(14)

(b)

\displaystyle\forall\alpha>0,\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{C}_{k,T}\left(1+\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\mu\right\rangle\right)}{\alpha}.

(2)

If
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
, respectively
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k+1},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
, the process
\left(\widehat{M}_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}

defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{0}\right\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}{\left\langle\frac{k(k-1)}{2}{\rm{id}}^{k-2},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{\left[\left\langle k(k-1){\rm{id}}^{k-2},X_{s}\right\rangle M_{2}(X_{s})-2k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s}

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
local martingale, respectively a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale. Moreover, if
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2k},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
, then

\left(\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a martingale in
L^{2}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}\right)
whose quadratic variation is given by

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2k},X_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}+k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-1},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}M_{2}(X_{s})\right.}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\left.2k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-1},X_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k+1},X_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s.

Note that the properties for
k=1
fail because of the term
M_{2}
in the expression of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}
. Note also that this results entails that
Z_{t}
is a continuous martingale.

Proof.

Step 1. Proof of (1)(a).
We prove only the case
k\geqslant 3
: the case
k=2
, which is simpler because some terms disappear, is treated in the same way. Let
t\in[0,T]
. We consider a sequence of functions
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
with compact support satisfying:

(i)
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
|g_{n}|\leqslant|{\rm{id}}|
,

(iii)

g_{n}={\rm{id}}
on
[-n,n]
,

(ii)

\displaystyle{\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\left\|g^{\prime\prime}_{n}\right\|_{\infty}}=0}
,

(iv)

g_{n}^{\prime}
is uniformly bounded on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

We consider the sequence of functions
\left(h_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
defined by
h_{n}:=\sqrt{1+g_{n}^{2}}
and we deduce from the properties of
g_{n}
that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
h_{n}
is a non-negative function with compact support,
that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime}=kg_{n}g_{n}^{\prime}h_{n}^{k-2}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime\prime}=k\left(g_{n}^{\prime}\right)^{2}h_{n}^{k-4}\left(h_{n}^{2}+(k-2)g_{n}^{2}\right)+kg_{n}g_{n}^{\prime\prime}h_{n}^{k-2},

h_{n}=h:=\sqrt{1+{\rm{id}}^{2}}
on the compact set
[-n,n]
and
h_{n}\leqslant h
on
{\mathbb{R}}
. We consider for all
A\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the stopping time
\tau_{A,\ell}:=\inf{\left\{t\geqslant 0\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{\ell},X_{t}\right\rangle\geqslant A\right\}}
. Noting that for all
t\in[0,T]
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\geqslant 3
,

\left\langle h_{n}^{k-2},X_{t}\right\rangle\leqslant\left\langle h_{n}^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle^{\frac{k-2}{k}}\leqslant\left\langle h^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle h_{n}^{k-2},X_{t}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},X_{t}\right\rangle\leqslant\left\langle h^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle
from
Hölder
’s inequality, we deduce from the martingale problem (
4
) that there exists constants
C_{1}(k),C_{2}(k,A)>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle h_{n}^{k},X_{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle+C_{1}(k){\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle h^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\right)+C_{2}(k,A)\left\|g_{n}^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty}.

By
Fatou
’s lemma we obtain when
n\to+\infty
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle h^{k},X_{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle+C_{1}(k){\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle h^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\right).

By
Gronwall
’s lemma, we deduce that

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},X_{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle h^{k},X_{t\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle\exp\left(C_{1}(k)t\right).

(15)

In particular, this implies that the sequence
\left(\tau_{A,k}\right)_{A\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
a.s. to infinity. Indeed, for all
\widetilde{T}>0
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{A\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\tau_{A,k}}<\widetilde{T}\right)\leqslant\frac{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},X_{t\wedge\widetilde{T}\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\right\rangle\right)}}{A}

which tends to
0
when
A\to+\infty
. We deduce by
Fatou
’s lemma, when
A\to+\infty
, the first announced result.

Step 2. Proof of (1)(b).
Let
\alpha>0
. From the martingale problem (
4
), we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\wedge\tau_{A,k}\right]}{\left\langle h_{n}^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\left\langle h_{n}^{k},\mu\right\rangle\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle\frac{\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime\prime}}{2},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\gamma\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime\prime},X_{s}\right\rangle M_{2}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(2\gamma\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime}\times{\rm{id}},X_{s}\right\rangle M_{2}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\wedge\tau_{A,k}\right]}{\left|\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}_{t}\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)\right|}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)

The
Doob
maximal inequality allows us to write

{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\wedge\tau_{A,k}\right]}{\left|\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}_{t}\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)\right|}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{5}\right)\leqslant\frac{5{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left(\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}_{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)\right)_{+}\right)}{\alpha}.

From the martingale problem (
4
) and the computations of Step 1, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\left|\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}_{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}\left(h_{n}^{k}\right)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle+2C_{1}(k){\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{T\wedge\tau_{A,k}}{\left\langle h^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\right)+2C_{2}(k,A)\left\|g_{n}^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle\left[1+\exp\left(C_{1}(k)T\right)\right]+2C_{2}(k,A)\left\|g_{n}^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty},

where we use the
Fubini-Tonelli
theorem and the relation (
15
). It follows, from
Markov
’s inequality, that there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\wedge\tau_{A,k}\right]}{\left\langle h_{n}^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C_{k}}{\alpha}\left[\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle\left[1+\exp\left(C_{1}(k)T\right)\right]+C_{2}(k,A)\left\|g_{n}^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\right].

By applying the dominated convergence theorem twice, successively when
n\to+\infty
then when
A\to+\infty
, there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}_{k,T}>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}{\left\langle h^{k},X_{t}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{C}_{k,T}\left\langle h^{k},\mu\right\rangle}{\alpha},

and thus the announced result.

Step 3.
\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)
is a continuous local martingale.
From the properties of
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, note that there exists a constant
\widehat{C}_{k}>0
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}},\quad\left|g_{n}^{k}\right|\leqslant\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k}
and
\left|\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right|\leqslant\widehat{C}_{k}\left(1+\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k-1}\right)
. It follows from the martingale problem (
4
), the properties of
\left(g_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and the dominated convergence theorem for conditional expectation that

\displaystyle\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\widehat{M}_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2}}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2}}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{0}\right\rangle-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{A,2}}{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-2},X_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}

\displaystyle\qquad-\gamma\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{A,2}}{\left[k(k-1)\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-2},X_{s}\right\rangle M_{2}(X_{s})-2k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s}

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale and thus
\left(\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
local martingale. When
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k+1},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
, using the inequality for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k-1},X_{t}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},X_{t}\right\rangle\leqslant\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k+1},X_{t}\right\rangle,

the same computation applies replacing
t\wedge\tau_{A,2}
by
t
to obtain that
\left(\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale.

Step 4.
L^{2}-
martingale and quadratic variation.
As soon as
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k+1},\mu\right\rangle<\infty
,
\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\in L^{2}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}\right)
as a straightforward consequence of
Hölder
’s inequality. It follows from (
6
) that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{\left(\left\langle g_{n}^{2k},X_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle g_{n}^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}M_{2}(X_{s})\right.}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.-2\left\langle\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)^{\prime},X_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle g_{n}^{k}\times{\rm{id}},X_{s}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the process

N_{t,n}:=\left[\widehat{M}_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)\right]^{2}-\left\langle\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g_{n}^{k}\right)\right\rangle_{t}

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
local martingale. As
\widehat{M}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)
is bounded on
[0,T\wedge\tau_{A,2k}]
for all
A\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(N_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2k},n}\right)
is a martingale.
From the relation (
14
) with
2k
, the dominated convergence theorem for conditional expectation implies as above that
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
a.s.

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{N_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2k},n}}=\left[\widehat{M}_{t\wedge\tau_{A,2k}}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right]^{2}-2\gamma\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{A,2k}}{\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2k},X_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right.}\\
+\left.k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-1},X_{s}\right\rangle^{2}M_{2}(X_{s})-2k\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k-1},X_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k+1},X_{s}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale and we deduce the quadratic variation announced.∎

2.3.3
Compact support

For all
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, we denote by
{\rm{Supp}\,}\nu
the support of
\nu
. The historical reference of compact support property of the original
Fleming-Viot
process is

[
10
,
Theorem 7.1]
where the authors proved that
{\rm{Supp}\,}Y_{t}
is a.s. compact for each fixed
t>0
. We will used a slightly stronger version based on

[
37
]
.

Proposition 2.8
.

For all
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})
, for all
\varepsilon>0
,
\bigcup_{\varepsilon\leqslant s\leqslant t}{{\rm{Supp}\,}{Z_{s}}}
is compact
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
a.s.
Further, if
{\rm{Supp}\,}{Z_{0}}
is compact, then
\overline{\bigcup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}{{\rm{Supp}\,}{Z_{s}}}}
is compact for all
t>0
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
a.s.

Proof.

It is proved in

[
37
]
that the support of the
\Lambda-
Fleming-Viot
process associated to a
\Lambda-
coalescent which comes down from infinity, is compact at all positive times. Our case corresponds to
Kingman
’s coalescent. In addition, they prove that, given that the initial condition
\nu
has compact support,

\overline{\bigcup_{0\leqslant s<t}{{\rm{Supp}\,}{Y_{s}}}}

is compact for all
t>0
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
a.s.
Markov
’s property then entails that, if
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})
,
\bigcup_{\varepsilon\leqslant s\leqslant t}{{\rm{Supp}\,}{Y_{s}}}
is compact for all
0<\varepsilon<t
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
a.s. Hence, the same is true for
Z_{t}=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}
. ∎

3
Uniqueness for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process

As for the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem, we will prove uniqueness to the martingale problem (
4
) by relying on the
duality method

[
17
,
9
,
21
,
23
]
. Additional difficulties occur in our case since bounds on the dual process are harder to obtain and the duality identity cannot be proved in its usual form. In particular, we can prove uniqueness only for initial conditions admitting finite moments.

3.1
Main result

Theorem 3.1
.

The centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem
(
4
)

has a unique solution if its initial condition has all their moments finite.

The reason why we need to assume finite initial moments will be explained at the end of Section
3.2.2
. In particular, we will see in Remark
6.5
that we cannot hope to prove uniqueness for more general initial conditions using our duality method.

3.2
Notations and outline of the uniqueness proof

3.2.1
Martingale problem for polynomials

In this section, we give an extension to the martingale problem (
4
) which will be useful to prove uniqueness of the solution of the martingale problem of the centered
Fleming-Viot
. The heuristic leading to this extension is given in Appendix
B
. Let us introduce, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star},\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
, polynomial test functions:

P_{f,n}(\mu):=\left\langle f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\cdots\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{1})\cdots\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{n})}}

(16)

where
\mu^{n}
is the
n-
fold product measure of
\mu
.

Definition 3.2
.

The probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}\right)
is said to solve the
centered
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem for polynomials
with resampling rate
\gamma
and with initial condition
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
, if the canonical process
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}(X_{0}=\mu)=1
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, and for each
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
,

\widehat{M}^{(n),d}_{t}(f):=\left\langle f,X_{t}^{n}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,X_{0}^{n}\right\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{d}{P_{f,n}\left(X_{s}\right)}\mathrm{d}s}\\

(17)

with for all
\varpi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)
and
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}\left(\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{d}{P_{f,n}(\varpi)}:=\left\langle B^{(n),d}f,\varpi^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\gamma\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\,=\,1\\
j\,\neq\,i\end{subarray}}^{n}{\left[\left\langle\Phi_{i,j}f,\varpi^{n-1}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\varpi^{n}\right\rangle\right]}+\gamma\sum\limits_{i,j\,=\,1}^{n}{\left\langle K_{i,j}f,\varpi^{n+1}\right\rangle}

(18)

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}-
martingale in
L^{2}\left(\widetilde{\Omega}_{d}\right)
where,

B^{(n),d}f(x):=\frac{\Delta f(x)}{2}-2\gamma\sum_{k\,=\,1}^{d}{\left(\nabla f(x)\cdot\varepsilon_{k}\right)\left(x\cdot\varepsilon_{k}\right)}

(19)

where
\varepsilon_{k}=\left(e_{k},\cdots,e_{k}\right)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}
and
e_{k}
is the
k^{\rm th}
vector of the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
,

\bullet

\Phi_{i,j}:{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n},{\mathbb{R}})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n-1},{\mathbb{R}})
, with
i\neq j
, is the function obtained from
f
by inserting the vector
x_{i}
between
x_{j-1}
and
x_{j}
when
i<j
and by inserting the vector
x_{i-1}
between
x_{j-1}
and
x_{j}
when
i>j
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,j}f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{j-1},x_{i},x_{j},x_{j+1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad i<j

(20)

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,j}f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{j-1},x_{i-1},x_{j},x_{j+1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad i>j

\bullet

K_{i,j}:{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n},{\mathbb{R}})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}^{2}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n+1},{\mathbb{R}})
is defined as

K_{i,j}f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},x_{n+1}):={\rm{id}}\left(x_{n+1}\right)^{t}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i,k}\partial x_{j,\ell}}\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant k,\ell\leqslant d}{\rm{id}}\left(x_{n+1}\right).

(21)

Recall that the centering effect in the
Fleming-Viot
process introduces additional terms in the infinitesimal generator (see (
B.2
)). These terms, involving second moments of the measure, cannot be included in the
B^{(n),d}f
term of (
18
) because they do not have the proper dimensionality when expressed as integrals with respect to
\mu
. This is why we write them in the form
\langle K_{i,j}f,\mu^{n+1}\rangle
. As will appear in Section
3.2.2
below, this term leads to the creation of particles in the dual process, contrary to the standard
Fleming-Viot
process. This prevents us to apply the usual arguments and explains why we can only prove a weak version of the duality relation in Theorem
30
below.

Theorem 3.3
.

For all
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, the probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}
constructed in
Theorem
2.4
, satisfies the martingale problem of
Definition
3.2
.

Proof.

We can deduce the result from the original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem for polynomials

[
23
]
given below, following exactly the same arguments as for the proof of Theorem
2.4
. The probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\Omega_{d}}\right)
is said to solve the
original
Fleming-Viot
martingale problem for polynomials
with resampling rate
\gamma
and with initial condition
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, if the canonical process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\Omega_{d}
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}(Y_{0}=\nu)=1
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, and for each
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}},{\mathbb{R}})
,

M^{(n),d}_{t}(f):=\left\langle f,Y_{t}^{n}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,Y_{0}^{n}\right\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FV}^{{d}}{P_{f,n}\left(Y_{s}\right)}\mathrm{d}s}\\

(22)

with for all
\varpi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})

{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FV}^{{d}}{P_{f,n}\left(\varpi\right)}=\left\langle\frac{\Delta f}{2},\varpi^{n}\right\rangle+\gamma\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{n}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\,=\,1\\
j\,\neq\,i\end{subarray}}^{n}{\left(\left\langle\Phi_{i,j}f,\varpi^{n-1}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\varpi^{n}\right\rangle\right)}}

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale. By

[
23
,
Theorem 3.2
]
, the solution
{\mathbb{P}}^{\rm FV}_{\nu}
of the martingale problem (
8
) is the unique solution to the previous martingale problem. ∎

3.2.2
Duality

Our proof of Theorem
3.1
is based on the
duality method
as proposed in

[
21
,
23
]
.
From (
18
), the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{{d}}
applied on the function
P_{f,n}
defined in (
16
) with fixed
f
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, satisfies the following identity:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm FVc}^{{d}}{P_{f,n}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle B^{(n){,d}}f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle+\gamma\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{n}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\,=\,1\\
j\,\neq\,i\end{subarray}}^{n}{\left[\left\langle\Phi_{i,j}f,\mu^{n-1}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle\right]}}

(23)

\displaystyle+\gamma\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{n}{\sum\limits_{j\,=\,1}^{n}{\left[\left\langle K_{i,j}f,\mu^{n+1}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle\right]}}+\gamma n^{2}\left\langle f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=:\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}^{\star}_{f}{P_{f,n}(\mu)}+\gamma n^{2}\left\langle f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle

We note that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}^{\star}_{f}
can be seen as an operator acting on the function
f\mapsto{P_{f,n}(\mu)}
with fixed
\mu
. The operator
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}^{\star}_{f}
can be interpreted as the generator of a stochastic process on the state space
\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}{{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}},{\mathbb{R}})}
. Following
Ethier-Kurtz
’s works

[
21
,
23
]
, this suggests to introduce a dual process
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
, of generator
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}^{\star}_{f}
and to prove a duality relation of the form:

\forall t\geqslant 0,\quad{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\xi_{0},X^{M(0)}_{t}\right\rangle\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\xi_{t},X_{0}^{M(t)}\right\rangle\exp\left(\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{M^{2}(u)\mathrm{d}u}\right)\right)

(24)

where
M:=\left(M(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is a
Markov
’s birth and death process in
{\mathbb{N}}
whose transition rates
q_{i,j}
from
i
to
j
are given by:

{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(1)}}}}}\ \ q_{n,n+1}=\gamma n^{2}\qquad\quad{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(2)}}}}}\ \ q_{n,n-1}=\gamma n(n-1)\qquad\quad{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(3)}}}}}\ \ q_{i,j}=0\ {\rm{otherwise}}.

It is known that the relation (
24
) implies uniqueness
[
26
,
Theorem 4.4.2]
. However in our situation, it is difficult to obtain the strong version (
24
). For technical reasons, we will obtain only a weakened version. Therefore, the proof will be divided in two large steps.

Step 1. Construction of the dual process
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
.
The relation (
23
) suggests that the dual process
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
jumps, for all
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
from
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}},{\mathbb{R}})
to
K_{i,j}f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n+1}},{\mathbb{R}})
at rate
\gamma
and if
i\neq j
, from
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}},{\mathbb{R}})
to
\Phi_{i,j}f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n-1}},{\mathbb{R}})
at rate
\gamma
. Moreover, note that if
n=1
, the dual process can only jump from
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
to
K_{i,j}f
. Between jumps, this dual process evolves according to the semi-group of operator
\left(T^{(n){,d}}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
associated to the generator
B^{(n){,d}}
given by (
19
). We will give in Section
6
an explicit expression of the semi-group
\left(T^{(n){,d}}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined as an integral against Gaussian kernels. We define the dual process as follows:

Definition 3.4
.

For all
M(0)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, for all
\xi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{M(0)}},{\mathbb{R}})
,

\xi_{t}:=T^{\left(M\left(\tau_{n}\right)\right){,d}}\left(t-\tau_{n}\right)\Lambda_{n}T^{\left(M\left(\tau_{n-1}\right)\right){,d}}\left(\tau_{n}-\tau_{n-1}\right)\Lambda_{n-1}\cdots\Lambda_{1}T^{\left(M\left(0\right)\right){,d}}\left(\tau_{1}\right)\xi_{0},\\
\tau_{n}\leqslant t<\tau_{n+1},\ n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(25)

where
\left(\tau_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is the sequence of jump times of the birth-death process
M
with
\tau_{0}=0
and where
\left(\Lambda_{n}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a sequence of random operators. These are conditionally independent given
M
and satisfy for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n\geqslant 1
and
1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\Lambda_{k}=\Phi_{i,j}\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-19.91684pt\right.\left\{M\left(\tau_{k}^{-}\right)=n,M\left(\tau_{k}\right)=n-1\right\}\right)=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}

(26)

and for all
n\geqslant 1
and
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\Lambda_{k}=K_{i,j}\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-19.91684pt\right.\left\{M\left(\tau_{k}^{-}\right)=n,M\left(\tau_{k}\right)=n+1\right\}\right)=\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

(27)

Moreover, the random times
\left(\tau_{k}-\tau_{k-1}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
are independent conditionally to
M\left(\tau_{k-1}\right)=n
and of exponential law of parameter
\gamma n^{2}+\gamma n(n-1)
.

Note that particle creation for the centered
Fleming-Viot
dual process is possible unlike the original
Fleming-Viot
dual process. It comes from the
K_{i,j}
operator which has appeared under the centering effect. Note that these terms correspond to the ones with the factor
\langle{\rm{id}}_{i}\times{\rm{id}}_{j},\mu\rangle
,
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
in (
5
) or
M_{2}(\mu)
factor in (
7
). Indeed, for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}(({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
and all
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\sum_{i,j\,=\,1}^{n}{\left\langle K_{i,j}f,\mu^{n+1}\right\rangle}=\sum_{i,j\,=\,1}^{n}{\sum_{k,\ell\,=\,1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{x_{i,k}x_{j,\ell}}^{2}f,\mu^{n}\right\rangle\left\langle{\rm{id}}_{k}\times{\rm{id}}_{\ell},\mu\right\rangle}}

The terms
\Phi_{i,j}
are present both in the original and centered
Fleming-Viot
. Because of the operators
K_{i,j}
, difficulties will arise to get bounds on the dual process (see Section
6.2
). Note also that
M
is a non-explosive process:

\forall T>0,\qquad{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{M(t)}<+\infty\right)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\tau_{n}}=+\infty\right)=1.

(28)

Indeed, we note that for the choice of
\mu_{i}:=\gamma i(i-1)
and
\lambda_{i}:=\gamma i^{2}
,
i\geqslant 1
in

[
1
,
Theorem 2.2.]
, we have

\frac{\mu_{i}\cdots\mu_{2}}{\lambda_{i}\cdots\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}}=\frac{1}{2\gamma i}>0,

so that

\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1}{\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}+\frac{\mu_{i}}{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-1}}+\cdots+\frac{\mu_{i}\cdots\mu_{2}}{\lambda_{i}\cdots\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}}\right)}\geqslant\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1}{\frac{\mu_{i}\cdots\mu_{2}}{\lambda_{i}\cdots\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}}}=+\infty.

Hence,
M
is non-explosive.

Step 2. Weakened duality relation.
We consider fixed
M(0)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
\xi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{M(0)}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
a stochastic process whose law
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}
is a solution of the martingale problem (
4
) with
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
. We introduce a dual process
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
independent of
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
built on the same probability space (enlarging it if necessary). We shall denote by
{\mathbb{P}}_{(\mu,\xi_{0})}
, the law of
\left(\left(X_{t},\xi_{t}\right)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
on this probability space. For any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we introduce the stopping time

\theta_{k}:=\inf{\left\{t\geqslant 0\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|M(t)\geqslant k\quad{\rm{or}}\quad\exists s\in[0,t],\ \left\langle\xi_{s},X_{t-s}^{M(s)}\right\rangle\geqslant k\right\}}.

(29)

Theorem 3.5
.

Given any
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
,
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
as above, we have the
weakened duality identity
: for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and any stopping time
\theta
such that
\theta\leqslant\theta_{k}
,

\displaystyle\forall t\geqslant 0,\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(\left\langle\xi_{0},X^{M(0)}_{t\wedge\theta}\right\rangle\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(\left\langle\xi_{t\wedge\theta},X_{0}^{M(t\wedge\theta)}\right\rangle\exp\left(\gamma\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta}{M^{2}(u)\mathrm{d}u}\right)\right).

(30)

Note that this result holds true for any initial measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
.
The stopping time
\theta_{k}
ensures that each of the quantities involved in (
30
) are bounded and thus that their expectations are finite. This is where we need stronger assumptions on
\mu
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume that
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
has all its moments finite. Then, the stopping time
\theta_{k}
defined by
(
29
)
satisfies
\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\theta_{k}}=+\infty
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}-{\rm{a.s}}
.

We will see in Remark
6.5
that the assumption on
\mu
is optimal in the following sense: even if
\xi_{0}
is bounded,
\xi_{t}
may have polynomial growth of any exponent
k
in some direction of
{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{M(t)}
so that
\left\langle\left\|\xi_{t}\right\|,\mu^{M(t)}\right\rangle
is infinite if
\mu
has infinite
k^{\rm{th}}
moment. This shows that we cannot expect to have
\theta_{k}\to+\infty
when
k\to+\infty
if
\mu
has not all its moments finite.

The proofs of Theorem
30
and Lemma
3.6
are respectively given in Sections
6.2
and
6.3
. Once they are proved, the proof of uniqueness can be completed as follows.

3.2.3
Proof of Theorem
3.1
from Theorem
30
and Lemma
3.6

We rely on the
Ethier-Kurtz
’s result

[
26
,
Theorem 4.4.2]
: to get the desired result, i.e. the uniqueness of the martingale problem (
4
), it is sufficient to verify that if we give ourselves two solutions to the martingale problem (
4
) satisfying the weakened duality identity, then they have the same
1-
dimensional marginal laws.

Let
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
\left(\widetilde{X}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
be two solutions of the martingale problem (
4
) with the same initial condition
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
which has all its moments finite. Let
\left(\xi_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
be the dual process with initial condition
\xi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{M(0)},{\mathbb{R}})
with
M(0)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
. We suppose that these three processes are built on the same probability space and independent of each other. We denote for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\widetilde{\theta}_{k}:=\inf{\left\{t\geqslant 0\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|M(t)\geqslant k\quad{\rm{or}}\quad\exists s\in[0,t],\ \left\langle\xi_{s},\widetilde{X}_{t-s}^{M(s)}\right\rangle\geqslant k\right\}}.

These processes satisfy, for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\xi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d}})^{M(0)},{\mathbb{R}})
the weakened duality identity (
30
):

\displaystyle\forall t\geqslant 0,\qquad{\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(\left\langle\xi_{0},X^{M(0)}_{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(\left\langle\xi_{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}},\mu^{M(t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k})}\right\rangle\exp\left(\gamma\int_{0}^{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}}{M^{2}(u)\mathrm{d}u}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(\left\langle\xi_{0},\widetilde{X}^{M(0)}_{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}}\right\rangle\right).

From Lemma
3.6
, since
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
\left(\widetilde{X}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
have continuous paths for the topology of weak convergence, we have
{\mathbb{P}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}-
a.s.,

\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\left\langle\xi_{0},X_{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}}^{{M(0)}}\right\rangle}=\left\langle\xi_{0},X_{t}^{{M(0)}}\right\rangle\quad{\rm{and}}\quad\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\left\langle\xi_{0},\widetilde{X}_{t\wedge\theta_{k}\wedge\widetilde{\theta}_{k}}^{{M(0)}}\right\rangle}=\left\langle\xi_{0},\widetilde{X}_{t}^{{M(0)}}\right\rangle.

Therefore, we deduce from the dominated convergence theorem and (
16
), that for all
\xi_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{M(0)},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
,

\forall t\geqslant 0,\qquad{{\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(P_{\xi_{0},M(0)}\left(X_{t}\right)\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{\left(\mu,\xi_{0}\right)}\left(P_{\xi_{0},M(0)}\left(\widetilde{X}_{t}\right)\right)}.

As the set of test functions
\left\{P_{f,n}\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1}}}\hskip-14.22636pt\right.f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n},{\mathbb{R}}),n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}\right\}
is
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)-
convergence determining

[
9
,
Lemma 2.1.2]
, it is
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)-
separating

[
26
,
Chapter 3, Section 4, p.112]
, it follows that for any
t\geqslant 0
,
X_{t}^{{M(0)}}
and
\widetilde{X}_{t}^{{M(0)}}
have the same law. In particular, for the choice
M(0):=1
,

[
26
,
Theorem 4.4.2]
ensures uniqueness to the martingale problem (
4
).
\square

4
Ergodicity for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process

In this section, we establish ergodicity properties with exponential convergence in total variation for the centered
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. Note that in the case of the original
Fleming-Viot
process, ergodicity fails without the centering property

[
23
,
Section 9.1]
. Standard duality arguments would provide weak ergodicity estimates (see (
1
) and

[
23
]
). However, using a coupling argument based on the
Moran
process and its relationship with the
Kingman
coalescent, it is possible to obtain strong ergodicity bounds (see (
2
)) as done below. In addition, this will provide an explicit construction of the invariant probability measure from the
Donnelly-Kurtz
modified look-down

[
14
,
15
]
.
For all
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, we denote by

\|\mu-\nu\|_{TV}:=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1}{\left|\left\langle f,\mu\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\nu\right\rangle\right|},

the total variation distance between
\mu
and
\nu
. The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

There exists a unique invariant probability measure
\pi
for
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and constants
\alpha,\beta\in(0,+\infty)
such that for all
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, for all
T\geqslant 0
,

\left\|{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(Z_{T}\in\cdot\right)-\pi\right\|_{TV}\leqslant\alpha\exp\left(-\beta T\right).

The main part of this section is devoted to the proof of this result (Sections
4.1
to
4.3
) and in Section
4.4
, we characterise the invariant measure of the centered
Fleming-Viot
process. In Section
4.1
we construct the centered
Moran
process and we establish its convergence in law to the centered
Fleming-Viot
process. In Section
4.2
we construct, backward in time, the
Moran
process, its centered version and we exploit its relationship with the
Kingman
coalescent in order to prove in Section
4.3
an exponential coupling in total variation for the
Moran
process. We finally deduce the main result announced by letting the number of particles go to infinity.

4.1
Moran
’s models and
Fleming-Viot
’s processes

In

[
17
,
10
,
27
,
9
]
, the authors construct the original
Fleming-Viot
process as a scaling limit of a particle process: the
Moran
process. The aim of this section is to construct the version of the centered
Moran
process and to establish its convergence in law to the centered
Fleming-Viot
process.

We consider the
Moran
particle process
Y^{N}
defined by

Y^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{X_{i}(t)}}

with state space
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, the set of probability measures on
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
consisting of
N
atoms of mass
1/N
. Moreover, if
\left(X_{i}(0)\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is exchangeable, then for all
t>0
,
\left(X_{i}(t)\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is exchangeable

[
23
,
Theorem 6.1]
. The infinitesimal generator of the
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-
measure-valued process
Y^{N}
is given for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star},f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}},{\mathbb{R}})
,
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
by

\displaystyle L_{N}P_{f,n}\left(\mu_{N}\right)

\displaystyle:=P_{\frac{\Delta f}{2},n}\left(\mu_{N}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\gamma N(N-1)\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}{\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}{\left[P_{f,n}\left(\mu_{N}-\frac{\delta_{x}}{N}+\frac{\delta_{y}}{N}\right)-P_{f,n}\left(\mu_{N}\right)\right]\mu_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)\mu_{N}(\mathrm{d}y)}}.

The first term of the generator describes the effect of the mutation according to the Laplacian operator. The second term describes the sampling replacement mechanism: at rate
\gamma
(the sampling rate) an individual of type
x
is immediately replaced by one of type
y
. Note that the population size remains constant over time.

We recall the following convergence result

[
9
,
Theorem 2.7.1]
: for all initial condition
Y_{0}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{X_{i}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
with
\left(X_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
exchangeable
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-
valued random variables such that
Y_{0}^{N}
converges in law to
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
as
N\to+\infty
, the
Moran
process
\left(Y^{N}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
converges in law on
\Omega_{d}
, as
N\to+\infty
, to the original
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined as the solution to the martingale problem (
22
).

We denote
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}):=\left\{\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-19.91684pt\right.\left\langle{\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2}},\mu_{N}\right\rangle<\infty,\ \left\langle{\rm{id}},\mu_{N}\right\rangle=0\right\}
, and we define the centered
Moran
process
\left(Z^{N}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
by

Z^{N}_{t}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}^{N}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t}^{N},\qquad t\geqslant 0.

The main result of this section is the following:

Proposition 4.2
.

For all initial condition
Z_{0}^{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{X_{i}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
with
\left(X_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
exchangeable
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-
valued random variables such that
Z_{0}^{N}
converges in law to
Z_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
as
N\to+\infty
and satisfying
\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2}},Z_{0}^{N}\right\rangle\right)}<\infty
, the centered
Moran
process
\left(Z^{N}_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
converges in law on
{\mathscr{C}}\left([0,+\infty),{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
, as
N\to+\infty
, to the centered
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
solution of the martingale problem

(
17
)
with initial condition
Z_{0}
.

A difficulty in proving this result lies in the fact that
\mu\mapsto\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\mu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\mu
is not continuous on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
because
{\rm{id}}
is not bounded. Hence we need to carry out several approximations and control carefully the approximation error. In order to prove this proposition, we need to introduce some notations. For all
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
-valued function
f
on
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
, the
Lipschitz
seminorm is defined by
\|f\|_{L}=\sup_{x\neq y}{\frac{\left\|f(x)-f(y)\right\|}{\left\|x-y\right\|}}
. We denote by

BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}):=\left\{f:{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left|\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-19.91684pt\right.\left\|f\right\|_{BL}\leqslant 1\right\}

where
\left\|f\right\|_{BL}:=\left\|f\right\|_{L}+\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}
For all
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, we denote by

d_{FM}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|\left\langle f,\mu\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\nu\right\rangle\right\|},

the
Fortet-Mourier
distance. Recall that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\right)
endowed with the weak topology is complete for the distance of
Fortet-Mourier

[
16
,
Corollary 11.5.5]
. Let
\Lambda
denote the class of strictly increasing, continuous mappings of
[0,T]
onto itself. For a given metric space
E
, we denote by
{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],E\right)
, the space of right continuous and left limited (càd-làg) functions from
[0,T]
to
E
. For
x,y\in{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
, we define the distance
d_{0}(x,y)
by:

d_{0}(x,y):=\inf_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{d_{FM}(x\circ\lambda(t),y(t))}+\sup_{s<t}\left|\log\left(\frac{\lambda(t)-\lambda(s)}{t-s}\right)\right|\right\}.

From

[
2
,
Theorem 12.2 and Remark of page 121]
,
\left({\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right),d_{0}\right)
is a Polish space when
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
is endowed with the
Fortet-Mourier
distance and the topology induced by the distance
d_{0}
is the
Skorohod
topology. The following lemma is a basic property of the original
Fleming-Viot
. It can be proved similarly as Proposition
2.7
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
T>0
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
fixed. There exists a constant
C_{k,T}>0
independent of
N
such that for all
Y_{0}^{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
satisfying
\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{k}},Y_{0}^{N}\right\rangle\right)}<\infty
, the
Moran
process
\left(Y_{t}^{N}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
satisfies

\forall\alpha>0,\qquad{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}^{N}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\langle{\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{k}},Y_{t}^{N}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)\leqslant\frac{C_{k,T}\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{k}},Y_{0}^{N}\right\rangle\right)}}{\alpha}.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

We want to establish that

\forall g\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}\left({\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right),{\mathbb{R}}\right),\quad\lim_{N\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\left(Z^{N}\right)\right)}={\mathbb{E}}\left(g\left(Z\right)\right).

Let
\varepsilon>0
. We consider the two following maps
F
and
F_{\varepsilon}
from
{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
to
{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
defined by
F(y)(t):=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},y(t)\right\rangle}\,\sharp\,y(t)
and
F_{\varepsilon}(y)(t):=\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y(t)\right\rangle}\,\sharp\,y(t)
where
h_{\varepsilon}
is a map from
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
to
{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
defined by

h_{\varepsilon}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}x&{\rm{if}}&\|x\|\leqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\\
\frac{\bm{1}}{\varepsilon\|\bm{1}\|}&{\rm{if}}&\exists i\in\{1,\cdots,d\},\ x_{i}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\\
-\frac{\bm{1}}{\varepsilon\|\bm{1}\|}&{\rm{if}}&\exists i\in\{1,\cdots,d\},\ x_{i}<-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\end{array}\right..

where
\bm{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
designates the vector whose coordinates are all
1
.

Step 1. Continuity of
F_{\varepsilon}
.
In this step, we want to establish that
