F_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}\left({\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right),{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)\right).

To obtain this, it is equivalent to prove that if for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
y_{n},y\in{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
and
\lim_{n\to+\infty}d_{0}(y_{n},y)=0
, we have
\lim_{n\to+\infty}{d_{0}\left(F_{\varepsilon}(y_{n}),F_{\varepsilon}(y)\right)}=0
. As,
\lim_{n\to+\infty}d_{0}(y_{n},y)=0
, there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\geqslant n_{0}
, there exists
\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda
satisfying

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{d_{FM}(y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t),y(t))}+\sup_{s<t}\left|\log\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}(t)-\lambda_{n}(s)}{t-s}\right)\right|\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}.

(31)

Note that

d_{0}\left(F_{\varepsilon}(y_{n}),F_{\varepsilon}(y)\right)\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{d_{FM}(F_{\varepsilon}\left(y_{n}\right)\left(\lambda_{n}(t)\right),F_{\varepsilon}(y)(t))}+\sup_{s<t}\left|\log\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}(t)-\lambda_{n}(s)}{t-s}\right)\right|.

Now, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle d_{FM}\left(F_{\varepsilon}(y_{n})(\lambda_{n}(t)),F_{\varepsilon}(y)(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle-\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y(t)\right\rangle},y(t)\right\rangle\right\|}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)-y(t)\right\rangle\right\|}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle}-f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y(t)\right\rangle},y(t)\right\rangle\right\|}.

On the one hand, as
f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, it follows that
f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle}\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
and thus

\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)-y(t)\right\rangle\right\|}\leqslant d_{FM}\left(y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t),y(t)\right).

On the other hand, as
f
and
\varepsilon h_{\varepsilon}
are in
BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left\langle f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle}-f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y(t)\right\rangle},y(t)\right\rangle\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)\right\rangle+\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},y(t)\right\rangle\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\|\left\langle\varepsilon h_{\varepsilon},y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t)-y(t)\right\rangle\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}d_{FM}\left(y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t),y(t)\right).

It follows from (
31
) that,

\displaystyle d_{0}\left(F_{\varepsilon}(y_{n}),F_{\varepsilon}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(1+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{d_{FM}\left(y_{n}\circ\lambda_{n}(t),y(t)\right)}+\sup_{s<t}\left|\log\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}(t)-\lambda_{n}(s)}{t-s}\right)\right|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Step 2. Control in distance
d_{0}
of the difference between
F\left(Y^{N}\right)
and
F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)
.
We consider the
Moran
process
\left(Y_{t}^{N}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
started from
Y_{0}^{N}=Z_{0}^{N}
and the original
Fleming-Viot
process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
started from
Y_{0}=Z_{0}
. In this step, we consider the events

\Omega_{\varepsilon,N}:=\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2},Y^{N}_{t}\right\rangle}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\Omega_{\varepsilon,\infty}:=\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\}.

As
\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2},Z_{0}^{N}\right\rangle\right)}<\infty
, it follows from Lemma
4.3
(respectively Proposition
2.7
) that there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}_{T}>0
, independent of
N
, such that
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}^{N}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,N}\right)\geqslant 1-\widetilde{C}_{T}\sqrt{\varepsilon}
(respectively
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,\infty}\right)\geqslant 1-\widetilde{C}_{T}\sqrt{\varepsilon}
). Moreover, on
\Omega_{\varepsilon,N}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle d_{0}\left(F\left(Y^{N}\right),F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{d_{FM}\left(F\left(Y^{N}\right)(t),F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)(t)\right)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\sup_{f\in BL_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{\left\|f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}^{N}\right\rangle}-f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle h_{\varepsilon},Y_{t}^{N}\right\rangle}\right\|_{\infty}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\|\left\langle\left\|h_{\varepsilon}-{\rm{id}}\right\|,Y^{N}_{t}\right\rangle\right\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\|\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2},Y_{t}^{N}\right\rangle\right\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon},

where we used the inequality
\left\|h_{\varepsilon}-{\rm{id}}\right\|\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{2}
.
Similarly on
\Omega_{\varepsilon,\infty}
,

d_{0}\left(F\left(Y\right),F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y\right)\right)\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Step 3. Conclusion.
We want to prove that for all
g\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}\left({\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right),{\mathbb{R}}\right)
,

\lim_{N\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\left(Z^{N}\right)\right)}={\mathbb{E}}\left(g(Z)\right).

Thanks to the
Portmanteau
theorem

[
2
,
Theorem 2.1]
, it is sufficient to prove this for all
g

1-
Lipschitz
. As
\left(Y^{N}\right)_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
converges in law to
Y
, we deduce that, for
N
large enough,

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y\right)\right)\right|\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Using that
g
is
1-
Lipschitz
, and the inequalities of Step 2, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\left(Z^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\left(Z\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F\left(Y\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{N}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F_{\varepsilon}\left(Y\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(g\circ F\left(Y\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}+2\left\|g\right\|_{\infty}\left[{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}^{N}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,N}^{c}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,\infty}^{c}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left[{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}^{N}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,N}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{0}}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon,\infty}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(3+2\left\|g\right\|_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{T}\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

The announced result follows and completes the proof. ∎

4.2
Backward construction of
Moran
’s process and
Kingman
’s coalescent

In this section, we exploit the well-known relationship between the
Moran
model and the
Kingman
coalescent to obtain in Proposition
4.6
a result of exponential ergodicity in total variation for the centered
Moran
model uniformly in
N
. The genealogy of a sample from a population evolving according to the
Moran
model of Section
4.1
is exactly determined by
Kingman
’s coalescent with coalescence rate
2\gamma
. The state of the population at the final time is constructed from the ancestral positions by following the genealogy and adding mutations on the genealogical tree of the sample according to a standard Brownian motion.

More precisely, on the probability space
\left(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\right)
, for any
T>0
, given the sample path
\left(k_{N,t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
of a
Kingman
’s coalescent in
{\mathbb{D}}\left([0,T],\Pi_{N}\right)
with
\Pi_{N}
the set of partitions of
\{1,\cdots,N\}
, given an independent family of i.i.d. standard
d-
dimensional Brownian motions
(W^{(B)})_{B\subset\{1,\cdots,N\}}
and given an independent uniformly distributed random permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\cdots,N\}
, we can construct the random variable

\widehat{Y}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{u_{i}}},

(32)

where for all
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,

u_{i}:=u_{i}^{\mu_{N}}:=x_{a(\sigma(i))}+\int_{0}^{T}{\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{\left(B\left(s,i\right)\right)}},

(33)

where
B(s,i)
is the block of the partition
k_{N,s}
containing
i
and where
a(i)
is the number of the block of
k_{N,T}
containing
i
, according to some arbitrary order (e.g. lexicographic).

The well-known backward construction of the
Moran
model

[
19
,
Section 1.2]
,
[
18
,
Section 2.8]
entails the following result.

Proposition 4.4
.

For all initial condition
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
and all
T>0
,
Y^{N}_{T}\overset{{\rm{law}}}{=}\widehat{Y}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}
where
Y_{0}^{N}=\mu_{N}
.

. The times

i\to j
indicates that

’s genealogy

to time

.

We give an illustration of this result in Figure
4.2
, where mutations are denoted by
“
”. Not all of them are shown for the sake of clarity. For example,

u_{2}=x_{1}+w_{4}+w_{3}+w_{2}
, where
w_{2}:=W_{T-t_{4}}^{(\{2\})}-W_{0}^{(\{2\})}
,
w_{3}:=W_{T-t_{3}}^{(\{1,2\})}-W_{T-t_{4}}^{(\{1,2\})}
and
w_{4}:=W_{T}^{(\{1,2,4\})}-W_{T-t_{3}}^{(\{1,2,4\})}
.

We can then construct the centered version of the random variables
\widehat{Y}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}
as follows:

\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\widehat{Y}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\right\rangle}\sharp\,\widehat{Y}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}.

Corollary 4.5
.

For all initial condition
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
,
Z^{N}_{T}\overset{{\rm{law}}}{=}\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{v_{i}}}

where, for all
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
v_{i}:=v_{i}^{\mu_{N}}:=u_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{u_{j}}
and
Z_{0}^{N}=\mu_{N}
.

process where

.

.

We illustrate this result with the Figures
4.4
and
4.4
. In Figure
4.4
, we can observe that
u_{1}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{1}+w_{5}+w_{7}
,
u_{2}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{2}+w_{5}+w_{7}
,
u_{3}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{3}+w_{6}+w_{7}
,
u_{4}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{4}+w_{6}+w_{7}
, so that

\displaystyle v_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left[w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{1}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{2}+w_{3}+w_{4}\right],\qquad v_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left[w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{2}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{3}+w_{4}\right]

\displaystyle v_{3}=-\frac{1}{2}\left[w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{3}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{4}\right],\qquad v_{4}=-\frac{1}{2}\left[w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{4}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{3}\right]

and for Figure
4.4
that
u_{1}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{1}+w_{5}
,
u_{2}=x_{\sigma(a(1))}+w_{2}+w_{5}
,
u_{3}=x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{3}+w_{6}
,
u_{4}=x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{4}+w_{6}
so that

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[x_{\sigma(a(1))}-x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{1}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{2}+w_{3}+w_{4}\right],

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[x_{\sigma(a(1))}-x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{2}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{3}+w_{4}\right],

\displaystyle v_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left[x_{\sigma(a(1))}-x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{3}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{4}\right],

\displaystyle v_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left[x_{\sigma(a(1))}-x_{\sigma(a(3))}+w_{5}-w_{6}\right]+\frac{3}{4}w_{4}-\frac{1}{4}\left[w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{3}\right].

We notice that, when there is just one ancestral lineage as in Figure
4.4
, the random variable
\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{4}}_{T}
does not depend on the ancestral positions
x_{1},\cdots,x_{4}
: this is the centering effect. In general, when
n=\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1
,

v_{i}=\int_{0}^{T}{\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{\left(B\left(s^{-},i\right)\right)}}-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{\int_{0}^{T}{\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{\left(B\left(s^{-},j\right)\right)}}}.

(34)

This property is fundamental to implement coupling arguments leading to strong ergodicity.

4.3
Coupling arguments with two distinct initial conditions and proof of Theorem
4.1

In this section, we want to couple centered
Moran
’s processes from different initial conditions but with the same
Kingman
genealogy and the same mutations in order to establish the following exponential ergodicity result. Then, we prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition 4.6
.

For all
\mu_{N},\nu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, for all
T\geqslant 0
, there exist constants
\alpha,\beta\in(0,+\infty)
, independent of
\mu_{N},\nu_{N},T
and
N
such that

\left\|\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\in\cdot\right)-\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\nu_{N}}\in\cdot\right)\right\|_{TV}\leqslant\alpha\exp\left(-\beta T\right).

In particular, for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
there exists a unique invariant probability measure
\pi_{N}
for the centered
Moran
process
\left(Z_{t}^{N}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
such that for all
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, for all
T\geqslant 0
,

\left\|{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(Z_{T}^{N}\in\cdot\right)-\pi_{N}\right\|_{TV}\leqslant\alpha\exp\left(-\beta T\right).

Remark 4.7
.

The previous result is true for all deterministic initial conditions, so also for any random initial conditions.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

Step 1. Coupling.
Let us denote
\mu_{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{x_{i}}}
and
\nu_{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{y_{i}}}
.
We construct the random variables
\widehat{Y}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}
and
\widehat{Y}^{N,\nu_{N}}_{T}
as in (
32
) from the same realisations of
\left(k_{N,t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
,
(W^{(B)})_{B\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}}
and
\sigma
.
This allows us to construct on the same probability space the two random variables

\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{v_{i}^{\mu_{N}}}}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\nu_{N}}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{v_{i}^{\nu_{N}}}}

such that
Z^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}\overset{{\rm{law}}}{=}\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}
and
Z^{N,\nu_{N}}_{T}\overset{{\rm{law}}}{=}\widehat{Z}^{N,\nu_{N}}_{T}
.

Step 2. Control in total variation.
From (
34
), on the event
\left\{\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}
, we have that for all
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
v_{i}^{\mu_{N}}=v_{i}^{\nu_{N}}
a.s. and from

[
36
]
we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\in\cdot\right)-\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\nu_{N}}\in\cdot\right)\right\|_{TV}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\neq\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\nu_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-{\mathbb{K}}_{N,T}\left(\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right)

where we denote by
{\mathbb{K}}_{N,T}
the law of the
Kingman

N-
coalescent with coalescence rate
2\gamma
on
[0,T]
. We denote by
H_{N}:=\sum_{k\,=\,2}^{N}{T_{k}}
the height of the
Kingman

N-
coalescent where
\left(T_{k}\right)_{2\leqslant k\leqslant N}
are independent random variables such that
T_{k}
follows an exponential law of parameter
{2\gamma}\binom{k}{2}

[
18
,
Lemma 2.20]
. Now,
{\mathbb{K}}_{N,T}\left(\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right)\geqslant{\mathbb{K}}_{\infty,T}\left(\left|k_{\infty,T}\right|=1\right)={\mathbb{K}}_{\infty,T}\left(H_{\infty}\leqslant T\right)
and by the exponential
Tchebychev
inequality we have

{\mathbb{K}}_{\infty,T}\left(H_{\infty}>T\right)\leqslant\inf_{\lambda\,\in\,(0,{2\gamma})}{\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\exp\left(\lambda H_{\infty}\right)\right)}{\exp\left(\lambda T\right)}}.

Note that for all
\lambda\in\,(0,{2\gamma})
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left(\exp\left(\lambda H_{\infty}\right)\right)=\prod\limits_{k\,=\,2}^{+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\exp\left(\lambda T_{k}\right)\right)}=\frac{{2\gamma}}{{2\gamma}-\lambda}\prod\limits_{k\,=\,3}^{+\infty}{\frac{1}{1-\frac{\lambda}{\gamma k(k-1)}}},

(35)

where the last product is convergent. We deduce that

{\mathbb{K}}_{\infty,T}\left(H_{\infty}>T\right)\leqslant C\inf_{\lambda\,\in\,(0,{2\gamma})}{\frac{1}{({2\gamma}-\lambda)\exp(\lambda T)}}={2}C\gamma\exp(1)T\exp(-{2\gamma}T),

where
C:=\prod_{k\,=\,3}^{+\infty}{\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\gamma k(k-1)}}}
. The result follows for
\alpha:={2}C\gamma\exp(1)T
and
\beta:={2\gamma}
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Classically, it is sufficient to check that there exists constants
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that for all
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, for all
T\geqslant 0
,

\left\|{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu}\left(Z_{T}\in\cdot\right)-{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}\left(Z_{T}\in\cdot\right)\right\|_{TV}\leqslant\alpha\exp\left(-\beta T\right).

From
Lusin
’s theorem

[
49
,
Corollary of Theorem 2.24]
, Proposition
4.6
and Corollary
4.5
there exists two constants
\alpha,\beta\in(0,+\infty)
such that for all
\mu_{N},\nu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, for all
T\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1\\
f:{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}}\,{\rm continuous}\end{subarray}}{\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)\right|}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1}{\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)\right|}

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha\exp(-\beta T).

Now, let be fixed two deterministic initial conditions
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
and consider an i.i.d. sample
\left(X_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
of distribution
\mu
and an i.i.d. sample
\left(\widetilde{X}_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
of distribution
\nu
. Then, we construct two initial conditions
\mu_{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{X_{i}}}
and
\nu_{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{\widetilde{X}_{i}}}
such that
\mu_{N}
and
\nu_{N}
converge in law respectively to
\mu
and
\nu
. We define
\widetilde{\mu}_{N}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\mu_{N}\right\rangle}\sharp\,\mu_{N}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu_{N}\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu_{N}
such that
\widetilde{\mu}_{N},\widetilde{\nu}_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
. By construction, the assumptions of exchangeability of the random variables
\left(X_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
and
\left(\widetilde{X}_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
are satisfied,
\widetilde{\mu}_{N}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{N}
converge in law respectively to
\mu
and
\nu
and we have

{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},\widetilde{\mu}_{N}\right\rangle\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},\mu_{N}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\mu_{N}\right\rangle^{2}\right)=\left(1-\frac{1}{N}\right){{\mathbb{E}}\left(X_{1}^{2}\right)}.

Then, we deduce from Proposition
4.2
that for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}},{\mathbb{R}})
satisfying
\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
, for all
T\geqslant 0
,

\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}f\left(Z_{T}\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu}f\left(Z_{T}\right)\right|=\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{N}}\left(f\left(Z_{T}^{N}\right)\right)\right|\leqslant\alpha\exp(-\beta T)}

which concludes the proof. ∎

4.4
Characterisation of the invariant probability measure
\pi

The main result of this section is Theorem
4.13
of Section
4.4.2
, which gives a characterisation of the invariant measure
\pi
thanks to an adaptation of
Donnelly-Kurtz
’s modified look-down construction. Let us begin by giving a convergence result of the invariant probability measure
\pi_{N}
to the invariant probability measure
\pi
.

Lemma 4.8
.

The sequence of laws
\left(\pi_{N}\right)_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
converges in law to
\pi
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
.

Proof.

Let
T\geqslant 0
,
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
such that
\mu_{N}
converges in law to
\mu
. From Proposition
4.6
and Theorem
4.1
, we have for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}),{\mathbb{R}})
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle f,\pi_{N}\right\rangle-\left\langle f,\pi\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\left\langle f,\pi_{N}\right\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\right)\right)\right|+\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f\left(Z_{T}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f\left(Z_{T}\right)\right)-\left\langle f,\pi\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\alpha\exp\left(-\beta T\right)+\left|{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{N}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}_{T}^{N,\mu_{N}}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left(f\left(Z_{T}\right)\right)\right|.

The announced result follows from Proposition
4.2
. ∎

4.4.1
Characterisation of
\pi_{N}
from the modified look-down construction

We consider the probability space
\left(\check{\Omega},\check{{\mathcal{F}}},\check{{\mathbb{P}}}\right)
where we define the modified look-down process on
(-\infty,0]
as a population dynamics on the set
{\mathbb{N}}
of levels where
one individual is assigned to each level. To each pair of levels
(i,j)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
with
1\leqslant i<j
, we assign an independent
Poisson
processes
\left(N_{ij}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
with intensity
2\gamma
and to each level
i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, we assign an independent standard Brownian motion
\left(B_{i}(t)\right)_{t\leqslant 0}
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{-}
. Jointly with the modified look-down is constructed for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, the so-called
N-
look-down process whose evolution is given as follows:

B_{1}(t)
B_{2}\left(t\right)
B_{3}\left(t\right)
B_{4}\left(t\right)
B_{5}\left(t\right)
\check{u}_{1}
\check{u}_{2}
\check{u}_{3}
\check{u}_{4}
\check{u}_{5}
1
2
3
4
5
0
-t_{1}
-t_{2}
-t_{3}
-t_{4}
-T

Figure 4.5:
Graphical representation of the modified look-down process with
N=5
.

\check{u}_{1}
\check{u}_{3}
\check{u}_{4}
\check{u}_{2}
\check{u}_{5}
0
-t_{1}
-t_{2}
-t_{3}
-\check{T}_{coal}^{5}=-t_{4}
-T

Figure 4.6:
Kingman
’s genealogy
\left(\check{k}_{5,t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
under the modified look-down model on the left, tracing back from time
0
to time
-T
.

(1)

Birth/Death rule.
Each jump time
t_{k}
of one of the
Poisson
process
\left(N_{ij}\right)_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}
corresponds to a reproduction event at backward time
-t_{k}
. When the time
t_{k}
is the jump time of the
Poisson
process
N_{ij}
, we put an arrow from
i
to
j
as illustrated in Figure
4.6
which means that the individual at level
i
puts a child at level
j
. The offspring at level
j
adopts the current spatial position of its parent at level
i
. The parent level and position do not change. Individuals previously at level
\ell\in\{j,\cdots,N-1\}
are shifted one level up to
\ell+1
and the individual at level
N
dies.

(2)

Spatial motion.
Between reproduction events, individuals’ spatial positions at each level
i
evolve according to the standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
B_{i}(-t)
. As explain below, we will fix the position of the individual at level
1
at coalescence time to
0
.

Note that the
N-
modified look-down process is simply the first
N
levels of the
(N+k)-
modified look-down for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
. In other words, the modified look-down construction can be done with an infinite population as a projective limit of the so-called (infinite) modified look-down. From

[
14
,
15
]
, the genealogy
\left(\check{k}_{N,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
in backward time since time
0
of a sample from a population evolving according to the
N-
modified look-down is exactly determined by
Kingman
’s
N-
coalescent with coalescence rate
2\gamma
. In Figure
4.6
we give the
Kingman
genealogy associated to the
5-
modified look-down of the Figure
4.6
.

We denote by
a(i,t)
,
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
t\in(-\infty,0]
,
the ancestor level of the individual at level
i
at time
t
. For example, in Figure
4.6
, for all
t\in\,]-t_{3},-t_{4}]
,
a(5,t)=2
and for all
t\in\,]-t_{2},-t_{4}]
,
a(3,t)=1
. Let us consider the random variables

\displaystyle\check{T}_{coal}^{N}

\displaystyle:=\inf{\left\{T\geqslant 0\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|a(i,-T)=1,\ \forall i\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}\right\}},

\displaystyle\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\inf{\left\{T\geqslant 0\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|a(i,-T)=1,\ \forall i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}\right\}},

which can be interpreted respectively as the coalescence time (i.e. the first time where
|\check{k}_{N,t}|=1
) of the
Kingman

N-
coalescent
\left(\check{k}_{N,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
the
Kingman
coalescent
\left(\check{k}_{\infty,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
.
Note that, for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
\check{T}_{coal}^{N}\leqslant\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}

\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s. In the next proposition, we establish that
\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}
admits moments of any order.

Proposition 4.9
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
such that
{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left(\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}\right)^{k}\right)\leqslant C_{k}
.

Proof.

Note that
\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}=\sum_{k\,=\,2}^{+\infty}{T_{k}}
where
\left(T_{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 2}
are independent random variables such that
T_{k}
follows an exponential law of parameter
{2\gamma}\binom{k}{2}
. Hence the result follows from (
35
). ∎

We shall be interested in the spatial position
\check{u}_{i}
of the individual at level
i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
at time
0
assuming that the position of its ancestor at backward time
-\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}
is
0
. For example, if we assume that, in Figure
4.6
,
\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}=\check{T}_{coal}^{5}=t_{4}
, then the spatial position of the individual at level
5
at backward time
-\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}
, represented by the curve in bold in Figures
4.6
and
4.6
, is

\check{u}_{5}:=B_{2}(-t_{4})-B_{2}(-t_{3})+B_{3}(-t_{3})-B_{3}(-t_{2})+B_{4}(-t_{2})-B_{4}(-t_{1})+B_{5}(-t_{1}).

Similarly,
\check{u}_{1}:=B_{1}(-t_{4}),\check{u}_{2}:=B_{2}\left(-t_{4}\right),\check{u}_{3}:=B_{1}(-t_{4})-B_{1}(-t_{2})+B_{3}\left(-t_{2}\right),\check{u}_{4}:=B_{1}\left(-t_{4}\right)-B_{1}\left(-t_{2}\right)+B_{3}\left(-t_{2}\right)-B_{3}\left(-t_{1}\right)+B_{4}\left(-t_{1}\right)
. In general, we define for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, the random variable

\check{u}_{i}:=\int_{-\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}}^{0}{\mathrm{d}B_{a\left(i,t\right)}(t)}.

(36)

In view of (
34
), it is natural to introduce for all
N,i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,

\check{v}_{i}^{N}:=\check{u}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{j}}.

(37)

Let us define respectively the empirical distribution of
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
and its centered version by

\check{Y}_{coal}^{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{\check{u}_{i}}}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\delta_{\check{v}_{i}^{N}}}.

For any fixed
T>0
, for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
, let us consider
T_{ij}
the coalescence time between the individuals
i
and
j
at time
T
in the process
\left(k_{N,t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
and
\check{T}_{ij}
the coalescence time between individuals at level
i
and
j
at time
0
in the process
\left(\check{k}_{N,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The measure-valued random variable
\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}
has the law
\pi_{N}
.

Proof.

The proof consists in establishing for all
f:{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\to{\mathbb{R}}
measurable real bounded function,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{f(\mu)\pi_{N}(\mathrm{d}\mu)}
. From Proposition
4.6
, it is sufficient to establish for all
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, that
\lim_{T\to+\infty}{\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}\right)\right)\right|}=0
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a measurable real bounded function. Note that,

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right){\mathds{1}}_{\left\{\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}}\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(f\left(\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}\right){\mathds{1}}_{\left\{\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\left[\check{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|>1\right)+\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\left|k_{N,T}\right|>1\right)\right].

For all
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
, conditionally to
k_{N}:=\left(k_{N,t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
, on the event
\left\{\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}
, we have from (
33
) that for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
, the covariance matrix
{\rm{Cov}}\left(u_{i},u_{j}\left|\,k_{N}\right.\right)
is equal to
(T-T_{ij})I
where
I
designates the identity matrix of size
d
. Conditionally to
k_{N}
, on the event
\left\{\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}
, it follows from (
34
) that
\left(v_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\sim{\mathcal{N}}^{({Nd})}\left(0_{{\mathbb{R}}^{{Nd}}},\Sigma\right)
where
\Sigma
has the block decomposition
\left(\Sigma_{ij}\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}
where for all
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
,
\Sigma_{ij}
is the
d\times d
matrix defined by

\Sigma_{ij}:={\rm{Cov}}\left(v_{i},v_{j}\left|\,k_{N}\right.\right)=\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\,=\,1}^{N}{\left(T_{ik}+T_{jk}\right)}-\left(T_{ij}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{k,\ell\,=\,1}^{N}{T_{k\ell}}\right)\right)I.

In similar way as previously, we have from (
36
) and (
37
) that, conditionally to
\check{k}_{N}:=\left(\check{k}_{N,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
, on the event
\left\{\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}
, we have for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}

{\rm{Cov}}\left(\check{u}_{i},\check{u}_{j}\left|\,\check{k}_{N}\right.\right)=\left(\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}-\check{T}_{ij}\right)I.

(38)

Hence, conditionally to
\check{k}_{N}
, on the event
\left\{\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}
,
\left(\check{v}_{i}^{N}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\sim{\mathcal{N}}^{({Nd})}\left(0_{{\mathbb{R}}^{{Nd}}},\check{\Sigma}\right)
where
\check{\Sigma}:=\left(\check{\Sigma}_{ij}\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}
and for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
\check{\Sigma}_{ij}
is a matrix of size
d\times d
defined by

\check{\Sigma}_{ij}:={\rm{Cov}}\left(\check{v}_{i}^{N},\check{v}_{j}^{N}\left|\,\check{k}_{N}\right.\right)=\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\,=\,1}^{N}{\left(\check{T}_{ik}+\check{T}_{jk}\right)}-\left(\check{T}_{ij}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{k,\ell\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{T}_{k\ell}}\right)\right)I.

(39)

Hence, it follows that for all
\mu_{N}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,N}^{c,2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
,

\widehat{Z}^{N,\mu_{N}}_{T}{\mathds{1}}_{\left\{\left|k_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}}\overset{{\rm law}}{=}\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}{\mathds{1}}_{\left\{\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|=1\right\}}.

As established in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
4.6
,

\lim_{T\to+\infty}{\check{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\left|\check{k}_{N,T}\right|>1\right)}=\lim_{T\to+\infty}{\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\left|k_{N,T}\right|>1\right)}=0,

which concludes the proof. ∎

In the next proposition, we establish the exchangeability property of the family
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
which will allow to apply the
De Finetti
representation theorem.

Proposition 4.11
.

(1)

The family
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is exchangeable.

(2)

There exists a random variable measure-valued
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}:\check{\Omega}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
such that
\left(\check{Y}_{coal}^{N}\right)_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
converges
\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s.
when
N\to+\infty
to
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
which is equipped with the weak topology. Moreover, given
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}
,
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is i.i.d. of law
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

(1)
From

[
14
,
Proof of Theorem 2.2]
, it is enough to show for each
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}
is exchangeable. Let
\sigma:{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}\to{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
be a finite permutation, that is to say a bijection that leaves all but finitely many points unchanged. The well-known backward construction of the modified look-down process

[
14
,
15
]
entails that
\left(\check{k}_{\infty,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\overset{\rm law}{=}\left(\check{k}_{\infty,t}^{\sigma}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
where
\check{k}_{\infty,t}^{\sigma}
is the partition obtained by applying the permutation
\sigma
to
\check{k}_{\infty,t}
. Therefore, for any permutation
\sigma:\{1,\cdots,N\}\to\{1,\cdots,N\}
extended by
{\rm{id}}
to
{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, it is sufficient to prove that
\left(\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\left|\,\check{k}_{\infty}\right.\right)\overset{\rm law}{=}\left(\left(\check{u}_{\sigma(i)}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\left|\,\check{k}_{\infty}^{\sigma}\right.\right)
to obtain the announced result.

We define
\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty,\sigma}:=\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}\left(\check{k}_{\infty}^{\sigma}\right)
and for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
\check{T}_{ij}^{\sigma}:=\check{T}_{ij}\left(\check{k}_{\infty}^{\sigma}\right)
. Note that
\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty,\sigma}=\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}

\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s. and it follows from the fact
\left(\check{k}_{\infty,t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\overset{\rm law}{=}\left(\check{k}_{\infty,t}^{\sigma}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
that

\left(\left(\check{T}_{ij}^{\sigma},\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty,\sigma}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}\overset{\rm law}{=}\left(\left(\check{T}_{ij},\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}.

Hence, we conclude from (
38
).

(2)
As the family
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is exchangeable, the announced result follows from
De Finetti
’s representation theorem

[
33
,
Theorem 12.26 and Remark 12.27]
.
∎

We conclude this section with a corollary which will be useful to characterise the probability measure
\pi
.

Corollary 4.12
.

(1)

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the random variable
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}
satisfies
\left\langle\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|^{k},\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}\right\rangle<\infty

\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s.

(2)

The limit
\check{u}_{\infty}:=\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{j}}}

exists
\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s.
and satisfies
\check{u}_{\infty}=\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle
.

Proof.

(1)
For all
M\in(0,+\infty)
, let us consider
\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}
the truncation function of
{\rm{id}}
at level
M
defined by
\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}=\left\|{\rm{id}}\right\|
on
[-M,M]^{d}
and
\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}=M
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,\backslash\,[-M,M]^{d}
. By
Fatou
’s lemma, we obtain that

{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}^{k},\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\varliminf_{N\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}^{k},\check{Y}_{coal}^{N}\right\rangle\right)}\leqslant\varliminf_{N\to+\infty}{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\left(1+{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\|\check{u}_{i}\right\|^{2k}\right)\right)}}.

Now, classical moment results for Gaussian random variables show that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(G^{2n}\right)=\frac{(2n)!}{2^{n}n!}\sigma^{2n}
for
G\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})
. The announced result follows from (
38
).

(2)
From Proposition
4.11
, given
\check{Y}_{coal}^{\infty}
,
\left(\check{u}_{i}\right)_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
is i.i.d. Therefore, the announced almost surely existence limit follows from the Strong Law of Large Numbers. Moreover,

\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle=\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle}=\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{j}}}=\check{u}_{\infty},\qquad\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-{\rm a.s.}\qed

4.4.2
Characterisation of the invariant probability measure
\pi

We can now conclude with the characterisation of the invariant measure
\pi
: now we define the random variable
\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
as

\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle}\,\sharp\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}.

The following proposition establishes the convergence of
\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
to
\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}
. Let us recall the following well-known fact useful for the proof below: a straightforward adaptation of the proof of

[
9
,
Lemma 2.1.2]
allows us to obtain that the algebra of polynomials

{\rm{Span}}\left(\left\{{P_{f,n}(\mu)}\left.\phantom{1^{1^{1^{1}}}}\hskip-17.07182pt\right|f:{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}{\rm{\ uniformly\ continuous}},\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}),{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}\right\}\right)

is convergence determining in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})\right)
where
P_{f,n}
is given by (
16
).

Theorem 4.13
.

(1)

The sequence of random variables
\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
converges
\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s.
when
N\to+\infty
to
\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
for the weak convergence topology.

(2)

The random variable
\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}
has the law
\pi
.

Proof.

(1)
From the previous reminder, it is sufficient to prove that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, for all
f:{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
uniformly continuous,
\lim_{N\to+\infty}{P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)}=P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}\right)
. With an argument similar to the proof of Proposition
4.2
, we obtain that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
, for all
f:{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
uniformly continuous,
P_{f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\cdot\right\rangle},n}
from
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
to
{\mathbb{R}}

is continuous. Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
and
f:{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
uniformly continuous. Now,
\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})
from Corollary
4.12
and

{P_{f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle},n}\left(\check{Y}^{N}_{coal}\right)=P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad{P_{f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle},n}\left(\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)=P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)}

From Proposition
4.11
,
\lim_{N\to+\infty}{P_{f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle},n}\left(\check{Y}^{N}_{coal}\right)}=P_{f\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle},n}\left(\check{Y}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)

\check{{\mathbb{P}}}-
a.s. which concludes the proof.

(2)
Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
. As for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
and
f:{({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
uniformly continuous,

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}\right)\right)-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{P_{f,n}(\mu)\pi(\mathrm{d}\mu)}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left|P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{\infty}\right)-P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)\right|\right)+\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(P_{f,n}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)\right)-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{P_{f,n}\left(\mu\right)\pi_{N}(\mathrm{d}\mu)}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\left|\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{d}})}{P_{f,n}\left(\mu\right)\left[\pi(\mathrm{d}\mu)-\pi_{N}(\mathrm{d}\mu)\right]}\right|,

the announced result follows from Theorem
4.13

(1)
, Proposition
4.10
and Lemma
4.8
when
N\penalty\ \to\penalty\ +\infty
. ∎

4.4.3
Application: computation of the second moment of the invariant measure

We assume here that
d=1
. The last characterisation of the probability measure
\pi
is suitable to make explicit computations. The next corollary gives an expression of the second moment under
\pi
.

Corollary 4.14
.

We have
\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{c,2}({\mathbb{R}})}{M_{2}\left(\mu\right)\pi\left(\mathrm{d}\mu\right)}=1/{2\gamma}
.

Proof.

Step 1. Uniform bound in
N
of
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2k}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right),{k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}
.
In this step, we want to establish

\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2k},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)}<\infty.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
. From Proposition
4.11

(1)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2k},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left.{\mathbb{E}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\left|\check{u}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{j\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{j}}\right|^{2k}}\right|\left(\check{T}_{m\ell}\right)_{1\leqslant m,\ell\leqslant N},\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left(\left|\check{v}_{1}^{N}\right|^{2k}\left|\left(\check{T}_{m\ell}\right)_{1\leqslant m,\ell\leqslant N},\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}\right.\right)\right).

From (
39
) and Proposition
4.9
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left(\left|\check{v}_{1}^{N}\right|^{2k}\left|\left(\check{T}_{m,\ell}\right)_{1\leqslant m,\ell\leqslant N},\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}\right.\right)\right)=\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\check{\Sigma}_{ii}^{2k}\right)\leqslant\frac{(2k)!}{k!}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left(\check{T}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)^{2k}\right)<\infty,

and the announced result follows.

Step 2. Convergence result of
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)
to
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)
.
Note that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
M\in(0,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}_{N,M}+{\textbf{\rm{{(B)}}}}_{N,M}+{\textbf{\rm{{(C)}}}}_{N,M},

where

\displaystyle{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}_{N,M}

\displaystyle:=\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2}_{M},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)\right|,

\displaystyle{\textbf{\rm{{(B)}}}}_{N,M}

\displaystyle:=\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2}_{M},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2}_{M},\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)\right|,

\displaystyle{\textbf{\rm{{(C)}}}}_{N,M}

\displaystyle:=\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2}_{M},\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)-{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)\right|.

From the inequality
\left|{\rm{id}}^{2}-{\rm{id}}^{2}_{M}\right|\leqslant\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}M}\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{3}
, then the
Hölder
inequality, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}_{N,M}\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}^{2}-{\rm{id}}_{M}^{2}\right|,\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}M}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{3},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}M}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{4},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle^{\frac{3}{4}}\right).

From Step 1, we deduce that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,

{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}_{N,M}\leqslant\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}M}\left(1+\sup_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{4},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)}\right)<\infty.

In similar way, we obtain that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
{\textbf{\rm{{(C)}}}}_{N,M}\leqslant\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}M}\left(1+{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{4},\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)\right)
where
{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{4},\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)<\infty
from Corollary
4.12

(1)
. By the monotone convergence theorem, we deduce that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)=\lim_{M\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}^{2},\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)}
and
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)=\lim_{M\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|_{M}^{2},\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right\rangle\right)}
. From Theorem
4.13
, for all
M\in(0,+\infty)
,
\lim_{N\to+\infty}{{\textbf{\rm{{(B)}}}}_{N,M}}=0
. Hence
\lim_{N\to+\infty}{{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{N}_{coal}\right)\right)}={\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)
.

Step 3. Conclusion.

Note that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
,

\displaystyle M_{2}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)=\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{i}^{2}}-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\left[\sum\limits_{i\,=\,1}^{N}{\check{u}_{i}^{2}}+2\sum\limits_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}{\check{u}_{i}\check{u}_{j}}\right].

From (
38
), we have for all
i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,N\right\}
,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(\check{u}_{i}\check{u}_{j}\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left(\check{u}_{i}\check{u}_{j}\left|\,\check{k}_{N}\right.\right)\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\check{T}_{coal}^{\infty}-\check{T}_{ij}\right)
where
\check{T}_{ij}
is an exponential random variable with parameter
2\gamma
if
i\neq j
.
Therefore
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}_{coal}^{N}\right)\right)=\frac{N-1}{N}\times\frac{1}{{2\gamma}}
. By Step 2, we deduce that
{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{2}\left(\check{Z}^{\infty}_{coal}\right)\right)=1/{2\gamma}
which completes the proof. ∎

5
Proof of Theorem
2.4

The proof of Theorem
2.4
in any dimension
d\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
is identical to that in dimension
1
but is much more cumbersome to write. For the sake of clarity, only the proof in the case
d=1
is presented. We leave the details for the multidimensioanl case to the reader.

We divide the proof of the main result into 3 steps, each of which will constitute a section (Sections
5.1
to
5.3
). We recall that the aim of this proof is to prove that the law
{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},\nu\right\rangle}\sharp\,\nu}^{\rm FVc}
of the process
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
defined by

\forall t\geqslant 0,\qquad Z_{t}:=\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\sharp\,Y_{t},

under
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}
for
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is solution of the martingale problem (
4
).
By standard density arguments, it is sufficient to consider the case
F,g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{4}_{b}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
to obtain the announced result with
F\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
.
In (
11
) there are essentially two types of terms:
\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}-Y_{s}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle},Y_{t}-Y_{s}\right\rangle
,
j\in\{0,1,2\}
,
s\leqslant t
. In Sections
5.1
and
5.2
, we prove that the two previous quantities admit a
Doob
’s semi-martingale decomposition. In Section
5.3
we handle all the terms in (
11
) involving respectively the first and second derivatives of
F
. We end this section dealing with the different error terms involved in (
11
). By classical arguments, we can prove that the martingale involved in (
4
) is square integrable and we can compute its martingale bracket (
6
).

5.1
Doob
’s semi-martingale decomposition of
\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t}-Y_{s}\right\rangle
,
s\leqslant t

In (
8
),
M^{{\rm{id}}}(g)
is well-defined only for
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
. The expression makes sense for more general functions
g
. The goal of this section is to prove that, for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
M^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)
is the martingale part in the
Doob
semi-martingale decomposition of
\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},Y_{t}\right\rangle
. In particular,

\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle=M^{{\rm{id}}}_{s}({\rm{id}})-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}}),\qquad s\geqslant t_{i}^{n}\wedge t

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})

and let
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}
be a distribution on
\Omega
satisfying
(
8
)
and such that
Y_{0}
is equal in law to
\nu
. Let
T>0
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\star}
be fixed.

(1)

If
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\nu\right\rangle<\infty
, then there exist two constants
C_{k,T},\widetilde{C}_{k,T}>0
, such that any stochastic process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
whose law is
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}
satisfies

(a)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu}\left(\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},Y_{t}\right\rangle\right)}\leqslant C_{k,T}\left(1+\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\nu\right\rangle\right),

(b)

\displaystyle\forall\alpha>0,\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},Y_{t}\right\rangle}\geqslant\alpha\right)\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{C}_{k,T}\left(1+\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\nu\right\rangle\right)}{\alpha}.

(2)

If
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{k},\nu\right\rangle<\infty
, then the process
\left(M_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
defined by

M_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right):=\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},Y_{t}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},Y_{0}\right\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}{\left\langle\frac{k(k-1)}{2}{\rm{id}}^{k-2},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s},

is a continuous
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}-
martingale. Moreover, if
\left\langle\left|{\rm{id}}\right|^{2k},\nu\right\rangle<\infty
, then
\left(M^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a martingale in
L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)
whose quadratic variation is given by

\left\langle M^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}^{k}\right)\right\rangle_{t}=2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2k},Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{k},Y_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}s}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
2.7
. ∎

5.2
Doob
’s semi-martingale decomposition of
\left\langle g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle},Y_{t}-Y_{s}\right\rangle
,
s\leqslant t
,
j\in\{0,1,2\}

Equation (
11
) involves terms of the form
\left\langle g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle
with
j\in\{0,1,2\}
. We wish to express, each of these terms using the martingale problem (
8
). However, this leads us to consider quantities of the form

M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)

(40)

with
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, which are not well defined at the moment. Indeed, in (
40
) the input argument is a predictable
random
function of the process
\left(Y_{t}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
while the martingale problem (
8
) defines
M_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}(g)

only for
deterministic
functions
g
. Lemma
5.2
hereafter, allows us to give a precise meaning to (
40
) by extending the well-defined character of the martingales of (
8
) to predictable input arguments. The proof of this technical lemma, is based on classical arguments using
regular conditional probabilities
, that we recall in Appendix
C
for completeness.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
t^{\star}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a deterministic time and
h:\Omega\rightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
be a measurable function satisfying the following property:

\forall\,\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega,\qquad h(\omega)=h(\omega^{\prime})\qquad\qquad{\rm{if}}\quad\omega_{|_{\left[0,t^{\star}\right]}}=\omega^{\prime}_{|_{\left[0,t^{\star}\right]}}.

Then, the following process defined, for all
t\in[0,T]
, by

{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right):=M_{t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(h\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right)\right)\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right)-M_{t\wedge t^{\star}}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(h\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right)\right)\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right)

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega})
square integrable martingale whose quadratic variation is given by

\left\langle{\mathcal{M}}\left(\widetilde{\omega}\right)\right\rangle_{t}=2\gamma\int_{t\wedge t^{\star}}^{t}{\left[\left\langle h^{2}\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{s}\right),\widetilde{\omega}_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle h\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{s}\right),\widetilde{\omega}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}s}.

Lemma
5.2
with
t^{\star}=t
allows us to assert that (
40
) is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}(\mathrm{d}Y)-
martingale increment. Thus, we obtain for
j\in\{0,1,2\}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}-Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle=\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{(j+2)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}

\displaystyle\hskip 177.82971pt+M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)

where
\left(M_{s\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)\right)_{s\geqslant t_{i}^{n}}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}
square integrable martingale satisfying for all
s\geqslant t_{i}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle M_{\cdot\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)\right\rangle_{s}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{s\wedge t}{\left[\left\langle\left(g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle}\right)^{2},Y_{u}\right\rangle-\left\langle g^{(j)}\circ\tau_{-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle},Y_{u}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}u}.

5.3
Expressions of the terms of (
11
)

In the rest of this proof, we use the following notations to simplify the writing. We denote for all
s\geqslant 0
,
R(s):=\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle
. Our goal is to prove the following lemma:

Lemma 5.3
.

When the mesh of the subdivision
0=t_{0}^{n}<t_{1}^{n}<\dots<t_{p_{n}}^{n}=T
of
[0,T]
tends to
0
when
n\to+\infty
, we have the following convergence in probability

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}_{i}}}=\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Z_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left(\left\langle\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{2},Z_{s}\right\rangle+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma\left\langle g^{\prime\prime},Z_{s}\right\rangle M_{2}(Z_{s})}-2\gamma\left\langle g^{\prime}\times{\rm{id}},Z_{s}\right\rangle\right)}+{\rm{Mart_{t}}},

where
\left({\rm{Mart_{t}}}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale.

The proof of Lemma
5.3
is based on the following decomposition of
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}_{i}
(given by the expression (
12
)) which makes use of the
Doob
semi-martingale decompositions of Sections
5.1
and
5.2
.
We have

{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}_{i}=\sum\limits_{k\,=\,1}^{6}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{k}_{i}}

where

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{1}_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{2}_{i}

\displaystyle=M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right),

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{3}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\left[M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)\right]\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle,\phantom{\left(\tau_{-\left\langle Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t},{\rm{id}}\right\rangle}\right)}

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{4}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\left[M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)\right]\left[M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)\right],

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)\right]^{2}\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{6}_{i}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left|t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t-t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right|\left|M_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}({\rm{id}})-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}({\rm{id}})\right|\right).

Note that we used the following inequality

\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{(3)}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}\leqslant\left\|g^{(3)}\right\|_{\infty}\left(t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t-t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-{\rm{a.s.}}

to bound the term
\left[M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i+1}^{n}}({\rm{id}})-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}}({\rm{id}})\right]\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{(3)}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}
by
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{6}_{i}
. Our goal in the sequel is to write each of these six quantities as sums of finite variation terms, martingale terms and negligible terms and to study the limit of each of them.

5.3.1
Decomposition and study of
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{1}_{i}

Note that, for any
i\in\{0,\cdots,p_{n}-1\}
,

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{1}_{i}=\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}+\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}-g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}.

Using a argument of convergence of
Riemann
’s sums for the first term and Lemma
A.2

(2)
for the second one, we obtain in probability, that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{1}_{i}}}=\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Z_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{2},Z_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}.

5.3.2
Martingale contribution of
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{2}_{i}
and
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{3}_{i}

Note that
\sum_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)}{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{3}_{i},
is a stochastic integral with respect to the square integrable martingale
\left(M^{{\rm{id}}}_{s}({\rm{id}})\right)_{0\leqslant s\leqslant T}
. Since
F^{\prime}
and
s\mapsto\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle
are bounded, we deduce that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)}{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{3}_{i}

is
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale. The term

{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}:=\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)}{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{2}_{i}

is a stochastic integral with respect to a martingale which depends on
n
. However, the same argument as above applies because
\left({\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{n}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)
by Lemma
5.2
, hence uniformly integrable.

5.3.3
Contributions of
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{4}_{i}
and
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}

The contribution of the next two terms corresponds to the terms due to the centering effect in the martingale problem (
4
).

Study of the term
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{4}_{i}
.
Using
Itô
’s formula and the relation (
9
), we obtain that

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{4}_{i}

\displaystyle={\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{41}_{i}+{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{42}_{i}+{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{43}_{i}

where

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{41}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[M_{s}^{{\rm{id}}}({\rm{id}})-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}({\rm{id}})\right]\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{42}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[M_{s}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)-M_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{{\rm{id}}}\left(g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}\right)\right]\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{{\rm{id}}}\left({\rm{id}}\right)},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{43}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}\times g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s}.

Using the same arguments as for
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{2}_{i}
, we deduce, in probability, that for
k\in\{41,42\}
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{k}_{i}}}

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale. Moreover, we decompose the integral of
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{43}_{i}
in the following way:

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{43}_{i}

\displaystyle={\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{431}_{i}+{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{432}_{i}+{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{433}_{i}

where

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{431}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left\langle{\rm{id}}\times g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)}-{\rm{id}}\times g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{432}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)}-g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s},

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{433}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}\times g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle g^{\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(s\right)},Y_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s}.

Using Lemma
A.2
, we deduce, in probability, that for
k\in\{431,432\}
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{k}_{i}}}=0,

and we deduce from the convergence of
Riemann
’s sums that,
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
a.s. and hence in probability,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{433}_{i}}}=-2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Z_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle g^{\prime}\times{\rm{id}},Z_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}.

Study of the term
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}
.
As
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}
satisfies the following decomposition:

\displaystyle{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}=\left(\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[M^{{\rm{id}}}_{s}({\rm{id}})-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}})\right]\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{{\rm{id}}}({\rm{id}})}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\left.\gamma\int_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}^{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}{\left[\left\langle{\rm{id}}^{2},Y_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle{\rm{id}},Y_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}s}\right)\left\langle g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle

and proceeding as for
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{4}_{i}
above, we obtain, in probability, that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right){\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{5}_{i}}}=\gamma\int_{0}^{T}{F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Z_{s\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle g^{\prime\prime},Z_{s\wedge t}\right\rangle M_{2}\left(Z_{s\wedge t}\right)\mathrm{d}s}+{\rm{Mart}}_{t}^{(1)}

where
\left({\rm{Mart}}_{t}^{(1)}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale.

5.3.4
Study of the error term
{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(A)}}}}}^{6}_{i}

From the inequality: for all
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
xy\leqslant\frac{2}{3}\left(x^{\frac{3}{2}}+y^{3}\right)
and Lemma
A.3
, we deduce, in probability, that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}F^{\prime}\left(\left\langle g\circ\tau_{-R\left(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right)},Y_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}\right\rangle\right)\left|t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t-t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right|\left|M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}})-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}})\right|\\
\leqslant\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\frac{2\left\|F^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}}{3}\left(\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{\left|t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t-t_{i}^{n}\wedge t\right|^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{\left|M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i+1}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}})-M^{{\rm{id}}}_{t_{i}^{n}\wedge t}({\rm{id}})\right|^{3}}\right)}=0

and this completes the proof of Lemma
5.3
.
\square

5.3.5
Expressions of terms of (
11
) involving
F^{\prime\prime}
and error terms

Decomposing
{\rm{\textbf{(B)}}}_{i}
in similar way as for
{\rm{\textbf{(A)}}}_{i}
, and using similar arguments, we can prove the following lemma:

Lemma 5.4
.

When the mesh of the subdivision
0=t_{0}^{n}<t_{1}^{n}<\dots<t_{p_{n}}^{n}=T
of
[0,T]
tends to
0
when
n\to+\infty
, we obtain in probability that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\sum\limits_{i\,=\,0}^{p_{n}-1}{{\rm{\textbf{\rm{{(B)}}}}}_{i}}}=\gamma\int_{0}^{t}{F^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\langle g,Z_{s}\right\rangle\right)\left[\left\langle g^{2},Z_{s}\right\rangle-\left\langle g,Z_{s}\right\rangle^{2}+\left\langle g^{\prime},Z_{s}\right\rangle^{2}M_{2}\left(Z_{s}\right)\right.}\\
\left.\phantom{\left\langle g^{\prime},Z_{s}\right\rangle^{2}}-2\left\langle g^{\prime},Z_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle g\times{\rm{id}},Z_{s}\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}s+\widehat{{\rm{Mart}}}_{t}

where
\left({\widehat{{\rm{Mart}}}_{t}}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\nu}^{\rm FV}-
martingale.

From Lemma
A.3
, we deduce that the different error terms involved in the approximation (
11
) converge in probability to
0
.

6
Proof of the results of Section
3

Recall from (
19
) that
B^{(n),d}
is given for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left(\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
by

B^{(n),d}f(x):=\frac{\Delta f(x)}{2}-2\gamma\sum_{k\,=\,1}^{d}{\left(\nabla f(x)\cdot\varepsilon_{k}\right)\left(x\cdot\varepsilon_{k}\right)}

where
\varepsilon_{k}=\left(e_{k},\cdots,e_{k}\right)\in\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{n}
and
e_{k}
is the
k^{\rm th}
vector of the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. In this part, we justify the extension from the case
d=1
to the multidimensional case. To do this, we give a probabilistic interpretation of the semi-group
(T^{(n),d}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
, generated by the operator
B^{(n),d}
, using the
Feynman-Kac
formula: for any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}^{2}\left(\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
,

T^{(n),d}(t)f(x):={\mathbb{E}}_{x}f\left(X_{t}\right)

where
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is solution to the following SDE:

X_{0}=x,\qquad\qquad\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\mathrm{d}B_{t}-2\gamma b(X_{t})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad X_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{nd},\quad t>0,

where
\left(B_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}=(B_{t}^{(1)},\cdots,B_{t}^{(n)})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a
nd-
standard Brownian motion,
b(x)=\left(b_{i,k}(x)\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,\,1\leqslant k\leqslant d}=(\sum_{j\,=\,1}^{n}{x_{j,k}})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,\,1\leqslant k\leqslant d}
where
x:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})^{t}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}
and
x_{i}:=(x_{i,1},\cdots,x_{i,d})^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. We shall denote
X_{t}=(X^{i,k}_{t})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,\,1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
.

Note that, for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
,
Y_{t}^{(k)}:=(X_{t}^{1,k},\cdots,X_{t}^{n,k})
are
d
independent processes of semi-group
(T^{(n)}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}:=(T^{(n),1}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
, solutions to the following SDE:

\forall k\in\{1,\cdots,d\},\qquad Y_{0}^{(k)}=x_{k},\qquad\qquad\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{(k)}=\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{(k)}-2\gamma\left(Y_{t}^{(k)}\cdot\bm{1}\right)\bm{1}\mathrm{d}t,\quad t>0

where
\bm{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
denotes the vector whose coordinates are all
1
and
x\cdot y
denotes the scalar product between
x
and
y
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Considering a function
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left(\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
with product form
f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\right)=\prod_{k\,=\,1}^{n}{g_{k}\left(x_{k}\right)}
with
g_{k}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
, note that

T^{(n),d}(t)f\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\right)=\prod_{k\,=\,1}^{d}{T^{(n),1}(t)g_{k}(x_{k})}.

(41)

So, it is sufficient to study the case
d=1
since the general case can be deduced easily from (
41
) following the same method as below. We leave the details to the reader.

6.1
Study of a semi-group

From now on, we assume that
d=1
. In this section, we devote a specific study to the semi-group
(T^{(n)}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
generated by the operator
B^{(n)}:=B^{(n),1}
defined for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
by

B^{(n)}f(x)=\frac{\Delta f(x)}{2}-2\gamma\left(\nabla f(x)\cdot\bm{1}\right)(x\cdot\bm{1}).

(42)

In Section
6.1.1
, we provide an explicit expression of
(t,x)\mapsto T^{(n)}(t)f(x)
and prove that it is a strong solution to the semi-group PDE associated with
B^{(n)}
, by means of
Feynman-Kac
’s formula. With the aim of obtaining fairly fine bounds on this operator (see Corollary
6.2
), we give all the necessary details. In Section
6.1.2
, we give a MILD formulation of the martingale problem (
17
) using the semigroup
(T^{(n)}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
in Proposition
6.3
.

6.1.1
Construction of the semi-group

For any real vector-valued function
f
and
g
of
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, we denote by
(f\ast g)(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{f(t)g(x-t)\mathrm{d}t}
the convolution product of
f
and
g
. We denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{1,2}_{b}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
the space of real functions on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
with respect to the first variable and of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
to the second variable.

Theorem 6.1
.

The family of operators
\left(T^{(n)}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
defined as:

\displaystyle\forall t>0,\ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\qquad T^{(n)}(t)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{f(u)g_{t,x}^{X}(u)\mathrm{d}u},

(43)

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\hskip 7.11317pt\qquad T^{(n)}(0)f(x)

\displaystyle:=f(x),

where
g_{t,x}^{X}
is the density of the Gaussian distribution
{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}\left(m_{t,x},\Sigma_{t}\right)
where
\Sigma_{t}:=P\sigma_{t}P^{-1}
and
m_{t,x}:=P\mu_{t,P^{-1}x}=x-\frac{\left(1-\exp\left(-2\gamma nt\right)\right)}{n}\left(x\cdot\bm{1}\right)\bm{1}

with

\mu_{t,y}:=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\exp\left(-2\gamma nt\right)\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{pmatrix}\quad\rm{and}\quad\sigma_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}e_{4}(t)&0&\dots&\dots&0\\
0&t&0&\dots&0\\
\vdots&0&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&\dots&0&t\end{pmatrix},

where
e_{4}(t):=\frac{1-\exp(-4\gamma nt)}{4\gamma n}
and
P
is an explicit change of orthonormal basis matrix defined in the proof below, is a semi-group of bounded operators on
L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\right)
. In addition, for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
,

(1)

The application
(t,x)\mapsto T^{(n)}(t)f(x)
is of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
and is a strong solution of the PDE

\displaystyle\forall t\geqslant 0,\ \forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\qquad\partial_{t}u(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u(t,x)-2\gamma\left(\nabla u(t,x)\cdot\bm{1}\right)\left(x\cdot\bm{1}\right)

(44)

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\hskip 7.11317pt\qquad u(0,x)

\displaystyle=f(x),

(45)

and

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\textbf{\rm{{(2)}}}&\nabla T^{(n)}(t)f(x)&=&\left(\partial_{x_{i}}m_{t,x}\cdot\left(\nabla f\ast g_{t,0}^{X}\right)(m_{t,x})\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}^{t}\\
\textbf{\rm{{(3)}}}&\forall i,j\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\},\quad\partial_{x_{i}x_{j}}^{2}T^{(n)}(t)f(x)&=&\left(\partial_{x_{j}}m_{t,x}\right)^{t}\left[\left(f\ast{\rm{Hess}}\left(g_{t,0}^{X}\right)\right)(m_{t,x})\partial_{x_{i}}m_{t,x}\right].\end{array}

As we will see in the proof, everything follows quite directly from the
Feynman-Kac
formula, except the fact that
(t,x)\mapsto T^{(n)}(t)f(x)
is a strong solution of the PDE up to time
t=0
. This technical point will be useful for the MILD formulation and this is why we make a detailed proof.

Proof.

In view of the operator
B^{(n)}
given by (
42
), it is natural to define the semi-group
T^{(n)}(t)
using the
Feynman-Kac
formula: for any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}})
,

T^{(n)}(t)f(x):={\mathbb{E}}_{x}f(X_{t})

where
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is solution to the following SDE:

X_{0}=x,\qquad\qquad\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\mathrm{d}B_{t}-2\gamma\left(X_{t}\cdot\bm{1}\right)\bm{1}\mathrm{d}t,\qquad X_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\quad t>0

(46)

where
\left(B_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is a
n-
standard Brownian motion and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

In Step 1, we check that this definition of
T^{(n)}(t)
coincides with the one given in the statement of Theorem
6.1
. In Step 2, we verify that
(x,t)\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}_{x}f(X_{t})
is indeed a solution of the PDE (
44
) for all
t>0
. In Step 3, we treat the case
t=0
. In Step 4, we prove the announced expressions of the derivatives of
T^{(n)}(t)f(x)
.

Step 1. Change of basis in the SDE (
46
).
We consider the orthonormal basis
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
defined by
v_{1}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(1\ \cdots\ 1)^{t}
and for
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
,

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{i-1}{i}}\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{i-1},\cdots,\frac{1}{i-1}}_{i-1\ \rm{terms}},-1,0,\cdots,0\right)^{t}.

We denote by
P
the change of basis matrix from the canonical basis to the orthonormal basis
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
.
We define for all
t\geqslant 0
,
Z_{t}=P^{-1}X_{t}
, i.e.
Z_{t}:=\left(Z_{t}^{(1)},\cdots,Z_{t}^{(n)}\right)
where for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
Z_{t}^{(i)}:=\left(X_{t}\cdot v_{i}\right)
. It is standard to check that
W_{t}:=\left(W_{t}^{(1)},\cdots,W_{t}^{(n)}\right)
where for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
W_{t}^{(i)}:=\left(B_{t}\cdot v_{i}\right)
is a
n-
standard Brownian motion and that
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is solution to the SDE

Z_{0}=y=P^{-1}x,\qquad\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathrm{d}Z_{t}^{(1)}&=&\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{(1)}-2\gamma nZ_{t}^{(1)}\mathrm{d}t,&\\
\mathrm{d}Z_{t}^{(j)}&=&\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{(j)},&j\in\{2,\cdots,n\}\end{array}\right..

(47)

All coordinates in (
47
) are independent and solve a one-dimensional SDE whose solution is explicit (
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
for
Z^{(1)}
, standard Brownian motion for
Z^{(i)},i\geqslant 2
).
It follows that
Z_{t}
is a Gaussian vector of law
{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}\left(\mu_{t,y},\sigma_{t}\right)
.
Therefore, for any
t>0
and all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{Z_{t}}
has a density with respect to the
Lebesgue
measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
given by:

g^{Z}_{t,y}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\sqrt{\det(\sigma_{t})}}\exp\left(-\frac{\left[z_{1}-y_{1}\exp\left(-2\gamma nt\right)\right]^{2}}{2e_{4}(t)}-\frac{1}{2t}\sum\limits_{j\,=\,2}^{n}{(z_{j}-y_{j})^{2}}\right).

(48)

Since,
X_{t}=PZ_{t}
, we deduce that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and for all
t>0
,
X_{t}
follows the normal distribution
{\mathcal{N}}^{(n)}\left(m_{t,x},\Sigma_{t}\right)
, with density

\displaystyle g_{t,x}^{X}(r)

\displaystyle:=g_{t,P^{-1}x}^{Z}\left(P^{-1}r\right)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\sqrt{\det(\Sigma_{t})}}\exp\left(-\frac{(r-m_{t,x})^{t}\Sigma_{t}^{-1}(r-m_{t,x})}{2}\right).

(49)

Hence,
{\mathbb{E}}_{x}f\left(X_{t}\right)
coincides with (
43
).

Step 2.
T^{(n)}(t)f
is solution to (
44
) on
(0,+\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Without difficulty we verify that for any
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
g^{Z}_{t,y}
satifies the following
Fokker-Planck
PDE:

\forall t>0,\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\qquad\partial_{t}g^{Z}_{t,y}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{y}g^{Z}_{t,y}(z)-2\gamma n\partial_{y_{1}}g^{Z}_{t,y}(z).

(50)

We deduce from (
49
) that
